# Foyer — 项目约定

内存受限 GPU 上 agentic LLM serving 的准入控制研究。论文在 `paper/`（IEEEtran + LaTeX），
实验在共享的 2×RTX 3090 上跑，原始日志在实验机 `lab-3090:/data/xbw/turnstile/results/`。

## 证据纪律（所有工作都适用）

- **不许编造**引用、实验数字、数据集、结果。数字对不上就报对不上，不要顺手改圆。
- **论文里出现的每个数字都要能追到**代码、日志或 `paper/data/*.csv`。追不到的就标出来，
  不要留在正文里。
- **改 LaTeX 不许改科学含义**。措辞归措辞，结论归结论，两者分开说。
- 下结论前先看文件。涉及实验的陈述必须来自 `results/`、`paper/data/` 或实验机日志。

## 2026-09-24 新增的四条纪律（都是一天内犯的）

1. **不要把【还没跑】的实验写成已跑。** 一天内犯了两次：先在 §III 写
   "§results reports the test"（codex 抓），改完后在摘要、引言、模型节又写了
   五处现在时（子 agent 抓）。两轮审查独立命中同一处，说明这不是笔误是习惯。
   规矩：引用一个实验之前，**先去 results/ 或 paper/data/ 里确认它落地了**；
   没落地就写「已排队」并注明落地后再报。这是本项目第一条纪律
   （不许编造实验数字）的变体，而它比编造更隐蔽 —— 编造是假数，这是真计划。

2. **两条管线算同一个量，必须共用定义。** 表 I 的 Δ 来自 `gen_table.py`（读
   runs.csv，1 位小数），§V 的 Δ 来自 `frontier.py`（读原始日志，全精度）——
   同一条臂两边给 −0.55 和 −0.57。修法是让 `gen_table.py` **import**
   `frontier.py` 的 `FRONT` 与 `front_slo`，而不是各自实现一遍。
   **推论：任何「两个脚本都算过同一个数」的地方，都要检查它们是不是共用定义。**

3. **生成器的 run 列表不全会静默丢数据。** `gen_eviction.py` 的列表里有
   `foyer_hz1`，但本地镜像缺那条 run，于是重跑生成器时它无声消失 ——
   数据文件的集合与正文引用的集合不一致，而且没有任何报错。
   现在缺 run 会显式打印。**推论：凡是从「一批 run」生成表的脚本，都要对缺失
   报错，不能 continue 了事。**

4. **判据写在正文里，代码里也必须实现。** §V 的归因判据写的是「在跑数【相当】
   但步成本更高」，代码的 `elif` 只查了 τ，没查可比性 —— 于是任何跑得更多的臂
   都会被归成「步成本」，而那只是 τ 随 R̄ 的常规趋势。这条 bug 让归因表多算了
   五条。**推论：正文里每一条带阈值的判据，都要能在生成脚本里指出对应的那一行。**

5. **批量改动脚本中途 assert 失败 = 后面的修改全部没生效，而且不报错。**
   2026-09-24 犯过：一个 py 脚本里连着改六个文件，第五个的锚点没匹配上抛了
   AssertionError，脚本中断 —— 而第六、七处（92.6% 的分母说明、7% -> 7.6%）
   从未执行，我却以为改完了。是 `check_paper_numbers.py` 事后扫出来的。
   **规矩：批量改动用「每处独立 try/except + 最后汇总报告」，或者改完立刻跑
   `check_paper_numbers.py` 验一遍。** 只在前几处生效比全不生效更危险。

## 2026-09-25 新增：重跑替代旧测量时，生成器要有【已作废】名单

被争用作废的臂用同名 + `_r2` 重跑后，**旧名还在镜像里、也仍然满足集合判据**。
于是：

1. `gen_eviction.py` 的判据自检（`_derive`）会把「重跑替代」误报成「集合没说清」——
   需要一个显式的 `SUPERSEDED` 名单，写清每条为什么被替代。**判据要机械可重算，
   名单是判据的一部分。**
2. `gen_repeat_spread.py` 不排除的话，`bres`/`bres_r2` 同 policy 会归成一组，
   算出 10.1% 的「重复散布」—— 那不是 run-to-run 噪声，是【污染 vs 干净】的差。
   拿它当噪声上限，等于把这张表想量的东西量错。
3. **要区分「重跑替代」与「重复对」**：`cap3_hc`/`cap3_hc_r2` 是同一配置跑两次、
   两条都进正文（表 I 各占一行）；`foyer_bres`/`foyer_bres_r2` 是旧的那条被撤下。
   这个区别是语义的不是机械的，必须手写并写明理由。

## 2026-09-25 新增：lab-only 的表会随「实验机上有什么」漂移

`gen_gate_attrib` 的 all-rows 中位数在实验机上从 27.4 漂到 30.6 —— 一半是修了
「把在跑的 run 算进来」这个 bug（在跑的 run 日志长度是任意的），一半是集合本身长大了。
**规矩：论文引这类数必须同时给出集合规模**（正文写成「forty-two of them」）。
同一条护栏 `gen_projection.py` 早就有（`_done`），`gen_repeat_spread.py` 也有；
`gen_gate_attrib.py` 2026-09-25 才补上。

## 2026-09-24 晚 新增：本地镜像是【部分快照】，别拿它当全集

本地 `results/night/` 是从实验机 rsync 来的**子集**（当时 116 个目录 vs 实验机 145+），
而且**还留着实验机上已经清理掉的旧目录**（本地有 `load25c_cap2`，实验机上只有
`load25c_cap2r`）。凡是「**对 run 集合计数**」的生成器，在本地跑都会静默给出错的判决：

- `gen_concurrency.py` 的判据是「同时活着几条车道」——少一个目录就少一条车道。
  实测：本地跑把 `var_cap3` 从 **CONTESTED 判成 clean**（丢掉的 `_failed_4lane_*`
  正是它窗口里的那几条），而 var_cap3 是否受争用决定了 §「变负载实验有没有结果可给」。
  已加 `MIN_RUNS=140` 断言（镜像不全直接报错）+ 文档注明必须在实验机上跑。
  **同一条也适用于 `frontier.py --csv`（现在用 `TURNSTILE_RESULTS` 环境变量切换，
  本地跑时必须先确认 `pois200_*` 目录数与实验机一致）。**
- 新增 `scripts/lab/data_run_coverage.py`：把 `paper/data/*.csv` 里出现的每个 run 名
  拿去本地镜像里找，报「缺目录 / 缺 steps.jsonl」。它只管本地镜像的健康，不代表数据错。

**判断口径**：一个生成器的输出如果会随「目录里有哪些 run」变化（计数、排名、并发、
集合中位数），就必须在实验机上跑，或者先跑 `data_run_coverage.py` 确认镜像完整。

## 这个项目的三条血的教训

1. **手算的表一定会错。** `paper/data/decomp.csv` 曾有一行 busy/idle 来自另一条 run、
   wall 却是对的，两个数自相矛盾，图 2 因此画错，几个月没人发现。任何汇总表都应由
   `scripts/lab/*.py` 生成并带断言（见 `gen_decomp.py`：busy+idle 必须精确等于采样跨度，
   残差非零直接退出）。
1b. **从错的列抄数 —— 已经犯过四次。** 手抄汇总表时把相邻一列的数当成目标列：
   `pairB_cap2hc` 的 busy/idle 来自另一条 run、`foyer_hz2`/`foyer_hz0` 的 running
   抄错、表 I 的 rg4/foyfix 两行 Δ 用了中途读数、相关工作的「round-1+ 命中率
   89.8%」其实是 foyer_hc 的 **SLO**（真值 80.9%）。
   **凡是从表里抄一个数，抄完回头看一眼它在原表里是哪一列。**

2. **中位数会骗人。** `tool_wait` 中位 0.35 s 让整个项目以为会话不离开引擎；均值是 16.85 s。
   报分布的时候，中位、均值、p90、最大值一起报。
3. **"还没到" 和 "坏了" 长得一样。** 曾三次把「trace 的第一个会话还没到」误判成「实验卡住」。
   判断一个 run 是否正常，先看 `runner.out` 和它已跑时长，别只看 steps=0。

## 撤回记录

已经撤回的主张不许复活，除非有新证据。**与论文 §讨论的六条一一对应**（2026-09-24 对齐：
此前这里只列了五条，漏了「步成本的分母」，而论文从第一版起就把它算作六条之一，
是这个项目里唯一改过头条数字的一次）：

1. **步成本的分母**（τ 除以全时段均值却乘 busy 时间 —— 两个错误相互抵消，
   让两种测量方法看起来一致）；
2. `tool_wait` 中位 0.1s→**均值 16.85s**；
3. oracle 单调性 / 增长敏感度那条结论（立在一个已被判无效的臂上）；
4. 记账重复计费；
5. 空转率随并发单调下降；
6. 「驱逐→重算推高步时间」那次**错误的反驳**（被 `num_retracted_reqs≈0` 与
   `prompt_tokens_total == 计划 prefill` 反驳 —— 反驳本身被反驳）。

详见 `paper/claims_audit.md` 与 `docs/claim_register_20260921.md`。

## 引用红线

- arXiv **2608.30830** 自报对 MORI/Continuum 的 head-to-head 胜利**已被否证，禁引**；
  它的 41% goodput 损失与 ~10× 复活差可以引。
- **PrefixShield (2608.01657)** 已占 "admission responsibility" 术语，需避撞。
- 任何引用的标题、作者、年份都必须核实过才能进 `refs.bib`。**不要凭记忆写文献。**

## 硬件与运维

- 平时只用**卡 2 和卡 3**。卡 0/1 仅限 00:00–08:00 且确认空闲（`memory.used < 200 MiB`）。
- **别人占着卡就等，不要算剩余空间够不够。只在完整的卡上跑。**
- **不要同时占满四张卡。** 卡 0/1 可用时，总并发仍应 <= 2 条车道。
  2026-09-21 学到过一次（记在 `scripts/watchdog.sh` 注释里）：把四张卡全占满，
  长跑进程会被共享机器上其它租户的 reaper 杀掉。**2026-09-24 又犯了一次**：
  四条车道一起开，卡 0/1 的两条 run 在 02:36:46 / 02:37:00（相隔 14 秒，
  同一绝对时刻）双双被 SIGTERM，只跑到 679 和 624 步就终止，两条都作废。
  症状：`steps.jsonl` 里 `connection closed before message completed` 在某个时刻
  突然成簇出现，`status=FAILED`；server 日志里 `Subprocess scheduler_0 crashed
  with exit code -15`。**这个 SIGTERM 是结果不是原因，别去查谁发的。**
- **2026-09-24 追加：四条车道就算没被杀，数据也已经废了。** 审计（`scripts/lab/gen_concurrency.py`
  → `paper/data/concurrency.csv`）查明：同机同时 ≥4 条 run 且持续 ≥60 分钟，
  会把**逐请求 decode 间隔中位 τ₂ 抬高 25~40%**（30.9~33.3 ms vs 同族干净值 23.8~25.7）。
  2~3 条不抬（pairB_foyerhc 3 条跑 277 分钟，τ₂=23.8，正常）。
  **危险之处：引擎侧所有指标都看不出异常** —— `num_running_reqs`、占用率、驱逐量、
  重算量在这四条臂上与干净 run 不可区分，只有客户端实测的 τ₂ 露馅。
  四条控制器臂（bres/hz0/hz2/breshz1）因此作废并重跑（新名加 `_r2`）。
  详见 `docs/finding_concurrency_contamination_20260924.md`。
  **判据要按时长不按峰值**：`foyer_hz1` 峰值到过 4 条但只有 14 分钟，τ₂ 正常。
- **跑实验时必须挂 `gpu_watch.py`**（2026-09-24 用户提出，已接入 `run_matrix.py`）：
  池子守卫只管【启动那一刻】，跑到一半别人往同一张卡上挂进程会静默污染这一段的
  tau / SLO / 墙钟，而数据看上去完全正常。watcher 每 15 秒记一次该卡的显存、
  利用率、全部计算进程及其属主，输出到 `<run_dir>/gpu_watch.jsonl`；属主不是自己的
  标成 foreign。汇总用 `gpu_watch_summary.py <rundir>`，有它就可以写"这次测量期间
  该卡独占"。**这是低概率情况的便宜保险，不是对已有结果的质疑**：起跑时的池子守卫
  已保证池子严格对齐，且多次重复之间墙钟 CV 只有 0.9~1.8% —— 真被偷算力不会这么稳。
  所以早期 run 照常可信，不因缺这个记录而作废。
- KV pool 必须落在 `101,432 ± 20`，否则该 run 作废，不记录——SGLang 按启动时的空闲显存
  定池子，池子小了数字就不可比。

## 改文件

- **`open(path,"w")` 会在 write 之前就截断文件。** 2026-09-24 犯过：`s.replace(...)`
  后面多打一个逗号使返回值成了元组，write 抛 TypeError，但文件已经被清空
  （`sec_discussion.tex` 整篇丢失，页数从 15 掉到 12 是这个信号）。
  规矩：写完先断言 `isinstance(s, str)`，或者先写临时文件再 `os.replace`。
  征兆是**页数突然变少** —— 编译后页数掉了，先怀疑有文件被清空。
- 长文档改完后 `git diff --stat` 看一眼增删行数，与预期差太多就停下查。

## 语言

- 对用户回复一律用**中文**。代码、路径、报错原文可保留英文。
- TUI 里不要写 LaTeX 公式，用纯文本或中文名。

## git

commit message 里含反引号时**不要用 `-m`**，走 `-F 文件`——已因此静默丢词三次。

    29	\title{When Does Admission Control Pay?\\
    30	The Memory Tier Sets the Cost of Concurrency in Agentic LLM Serving}
    31	
    32	\author{\IEEEauthorblockN{Anonymous Submission}}
    33	
    34	\maketitle
    35	
    36	\begin{abstract}
    37	Agentic LLM sessions are long, multi-round jobs whose KV footprint grows every
    38	round, and on a memory-constrained accelerator the pool is the binding
    39	resource. Admission control therefore looks like the obvious lever: a
    40	controller that reads engine telemetry and admits on measured capacity should
    41	beat a static cap. We give a measurement that says how much
    42	room there is, and show what happens when you build against it. Throughput during busy time is
    43	$\overline{R}/\tau$---running requests over per-step cost---so raising
    44	concurrency by a factor $f$ buys $f^{1-\epsilon}$, where
    45	$\epsilon = \mathrm{d}\ln\tau / \mathrm{d}\ln\overline{R}$. Near zero, busy-time throughput keeps
    46	converting; near one it does not. It is a property of the workload \emph{and}
    47	the engine configuration, not of the controller, and what sets it is the
    48	memory tier. The trace bounds reuse at 75.5\% of its prompt tokens, and no
    49	configuration we ran reaches that bound---the best realizes 92.5\% of it.
    50	What the tier decides is how much of the bound survives added concurrency:
    51	device-only, realization falls from 92.5\% to 36.4\% as the running count
    52	rises from 1.00 to 1.84 and the shortfall appears as recomputation (8.6\,M to
    53	20.7\,M tokens); with a host tier the static points hold at 90.2--92.4\%
    54	between 1.25 and 1.73, and across the nineteen host-tier runs that completed
    55	this trace realization never falls below 72.8\%, while the \emph{device} cache
    56	misses more. Two configurations at the
    57	same running count and the same pool occupancy realize 79.0\% and 91.3\% of
    58	the bound, so it is not how full the pool is but what happens when it
    59	overflows---though that pair is two runs on two days, so we also have a
    60	capacity control in the queue that changes the pool size alone on one card; we
    61	report it when it lands. Our controllers run on the host-tier configuration, where
    62	$\epsilon$ stays below one across their whole operating range, so the ceiling
    63	we measure on device-only caps is not what bounded them. It bounds what added concurrency is worth, not what a
    64	whole run achieves---idle time and the SLO axis are separate. We measure $\epsilon$ on a
    65	memory-bound agentic replay (200 sessions, one 24\,GB GPU, a pool that holds
    66	four median sessions) and get $0.38$ to $0.61$ depending on which step cost a
    67	system can observe: every 10\% of added concurrency buys 4--6\% of throughput.
    68	A second, real agent trace (117 Claude sessions, same engine and pool)
    69	reproduces both measurements in direction, neither in level: realization falls
    70	there too, and the elasticity there also turns positive---rising to 1.0 where
    71	the main trace's reaches 1.15---at a different running count and by an amount
    72	our own perturbation study cannot pin down.
    73	
    74	The verdict on the controllers is negative, and we report it at that
    75	strength. (The criterion of \S\ref{sec:criterion} is a diagnostic and does
    76	not return this verdict; \S\ref{sec:criterion} is explicit that it does not
    77	explain the failure.) Nine controller variants spanning four routes, plus a
    78	reimplementation of an independent congestion-based controller, none is
    79	above the static Pareto front at the latency threshold interactive serving
    80	cares about---the best misses it by under a point, on the below side of our
    81	tie band, and the closest arm we repeated misses by 0.9 with its repeat at 5.6;
    82	a plain cap on resident sessions
    83	remains the fastest policy we measured. That is not an argument for admitting everything: with no control at
    84	all the median request waits 1{,}774 seconds for its first token, the prefix
    85	cache's realized hit rate collapses from the trace's planned 75.5\% to
    86	1.6\% (both are the runner's own end-of-run summary figures, on its own
    87	all-steps convention; \texttt{nocontrol.csv} carries them alongside four
    88	conventions we compute ourselves, which give 73.8\% and 1.9\% for the same
    89	run), and SLO falls to 3\%.
    90	The question is which selective rule, not whether to be selective. Two
    91	measurable effects, rather than one we can tune away, account for part of why
    92	our variants fail---the arm-by-arm attribution of
    93	\S\ref{sec:results:attribution} attributes three of the twelve controller
    94	arms we tested, leaves six with a step cost that no matched comparison can
    95	confirm, and leaves three as residuals we can measure and cannot yet
    96	explain. The single step-cost attribution it does make is on a run flagged
    97	for host co-tenancy but not measurably damaged by it, and we say which is
    98	which rather than counting it. They hold
    99	nearly half
   100	the pool idle while sessions queue---their own headroom estimate on the base
   101	configuration is 1.79$\times$ the truth (1.32--1.90$\times$ across the
   102	sixteen controller runs that finished on this trace, and 1.01--4.11$\times$
   103	over every finished controller run we logged)---and the per-step elasticity measured on the
   104	configuration they actually run stays below one across their whole operating
   105	range, so the engine had room they did not take: the ceiling we measure on
   106	device-only caps does not survive on the host-tier configuration the
   107	controllers use. The wall
   108	clock splits into busy and idle time; admission policy moves only the running
   109	count, and $\tau$ rises with it. The idle component (7.3--41.9\% of the
   110	sampled span) comes from gaps the tool waits set---an agent session issues no
   111	request while a tool runs,
   112	and that wait averages 16.85\,s measured over the controller's run, against the
   113	15.82\,s the trace demands---on the static cap of one the two match exactly, so
   114	we attribute the controller's extra 1.03\,s to its own admission delay---not
   115	the sub-second figure the trace's median suggests---though how many such gaps a run collects is partly the policy's,
   116	since a policy sets how many sessions stay live. On device-only configurations we can account for the rise token for
   117	token; on host-tier ones part of it is co-tenancy we found late and part we
   118	still cannot attribute, and we say which is which. We also report the six of
   119	our own claims we retracted along the way, and the boundary of the verdict: at
   120	a loose latency threshold one variant does cross the front, by a margin we
   121	cannot resolve against run-to-run noise.
   122	\end{abstract}

     1	\section{Background and Setup}
     2	\label{sec:bg}
     3	
     4	\subsection{Workload}
     5	
     6	We replay 200 coding-agent sessions drawn from the {\sc TraceLab} corpus
     7	\cite{zhu2026tracelab}. Each session is a
     8	sequence of rounds; a round presents a prompt of median 23{,}187 tokens
     9	(p90 51{,}064; maximum 89{,}436, which alone is 88\% of the KV pool), and
    10	between rounds the session waits on a tool before returning with a longer
    11	context. Arrivals are Poisson at $\lambda=0.04\,\text{s}^{-1}$ over an
    12	80-minute window, giving 999 steps in total. Two sessions of maximum size
    13	cannot be co-resident: their combined contexts (89{,}436 and 87{,}821 tokens)
    14	exceed the pool by 75\%.
    15	
    16	The tool wait is the load's defining property and the one most easily
    17	misread. Its \emph{median} is 0.35\,s, which invites the conclusion that
    18	sessions never leave the engine. Its \emph{mean} is 16.85\,s measured and 15.82\,s
    19	demanded, and the maximum is 300\,s; 21.8\% of the
    20	tool waits the trace specifies exceed ten seconds---18.0\% of the 799 round
    21	transitions, 14.4\% of its 999 steps---and 18.6\% of the measured gaps do. We verified that our replay
    22	harness honors the trace faithfully: across 799 round transitions the measured
    23	gap is 16.85\,s mean against a demanded 15.82\,s. On a typical transition the
    24	harness adds nothing---the median of the per-transition measured/demanded
    25	ratios is 1.001---while the ratio of the two means is 1.065, the difference
    26	sitting in the tail. The corpus's own characterization makes the same point
    27	qualitatively---it reports
    28	``diverse and heavily-tailed tool calls'' and calls these gaps
    29	``human-paced''---but gives no numeric mean \cite{zhu2026tracelab}, so the
    30	16.85\,s above is our measurement over the released trace, not a figure we
    31	quote from that paper.
    32	
    33	\subsection{Engine and the binding constraint}
    34	
    35	We use SGLang v0.5.10 \cite{zheng2024sglang} with Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct
    36	\cite{qwen2coder} extended to 96K context,
    37	on one RTX 3090 (24\,GB). KV is paged \cite{kwon2023pagedattention}; at \texttt{mem-fraction-static} 0.85
    38	the pool is 101{,}432 tokens. Hierarchical caching (HiCache), with a host tier of twice the device pool, is
    39	enabled on the configurations marked \emph{+HC} and on every \sys{} run; the
    40	five plain static caps run without it. We keep both because the host tier turns
    41	out to change the mechanism (\S\ref{sec:model}). Every run is guarded: if the pool does not
    42	come out at $101{,}432 \pm 20$---which happens when another tenant holds
    43	memory on the card---the run is aborted rather than recorded, because SGLang
    44	sizes the pool from free memory at startup and a shrunken pool silently
    45	produces incomparable numbers.
    46	
    47	The relevant consequence is that the pool holds only about four median
    48	sessions. Concurrency is therefore capacity-bound, not scheduler-bound, and
    49	this is the premise on which everything below rests.
    50	
    51	\subsection{The controller under test}
    52	
    53	\sys{} is an admission controller that reads engine telemetry and issues
    54	\emph{named} permits rather than a scalar cap: a session is admitted by
    55	appearing in a permit file, and the runner refuses to start a round for a
    56	session that is not listed. This design choice matters for the comparison,
    57	because it is what allows per-session actions at all---including the pause
    58	primitive we exploit in \S\ref{sec:results:gate}. It also means that a session,
    59	once admitted, holds its permit for its entire lifetime, so the controller
    60	never frees capacity by discarding a session's state---it admits fewer sessions
    61	instead. That is a statement about the \emph{policy}, not about the memory: the
    62	engine's radix cache still evicts parked prefixes under pressure, and we
    63	measure that it does and that they are recomputed (\S\ref{sec:model}). We
    64	return to this distinction in \S\ref{sec:discussion}, because it bounds how far
    65	our negative result generalises.
    66	
    67	Each control cycle (2\,s) the controller computes
    68	\begin{equation}
    69	  \Pi \;=\; \max(\rho, \textstyle\sum_i c_i) \;+\; \textstyle\sum_i g_i ,
    70	  \label{eq:proj}
    71	\end{equation}
    72	where $\rho$ is the engine's reported occupancy, $c_i$ the context length of
    73	live session $i$, and $g_i$ its forecast growth over the next
    74	\texttt{horizon} rounds. A session is admitted when $\Pi + n \le B$, with $n$
    75	the candidate's estimated need and $B$ the budget. The three terms are
    76	respectively the measured physical occupancy, a reservation for sessions
    77	currently parked in a tool wait (whose KV is evictable and therefore absent
    78	from $\rho$), and a reservation for future growth.
    79	
    80	\subsection{Baselines and the verdict criterion}
    81	
    82	Baselines are static caps: at most $N$ sessions resident, $N \in \{1,\dots,5\}$,
    83	with and without HiCache. We compare on two axes: end-to-end wall clock
    84	(time from the first submission to the last completion) and SLO attainment
    85	(fraction of requests whose time-to-first-token is under 10\,s).
    86	
    87	The verdict criterion deserves care, because ``faster than cap-$N$'' is not
    88	the question. The question is whether a policy reaches a point \emph{above}
    89	the static front. A point that lies merely between two static caps is not a
    90	win: mixing cap-$N$ and cap-$M$ by time slice approximates any point on the
    91	line between them. We flag this segment argument as an argument, not a
    92	measurement---we did not run the time-sliced policies, and carrying queue state
    93	and cache contents across a switch could bend the line. It is the criterion we
    94	apply, and it is the stricter of the two we could have used---stricter than
    95	comparing each controller against the discrete static points alone, because the
    96	line sits above them. We state the direction plainly rather than claim it as a
    97	caution: this choice makes our negative result \emph{easier} to reach, not
    98	harder. Our best arm is $0.93$\,pp below the line and would be $+3.7$\,pp
    99	against the nearest static point below it in wall clock (\texttt{cap3}+HiCache's
   100	first run, 81.1\% at 236.4 minutes; $+2.9$ would be the comparison against that
   101	anchor's repeat at 81.9\%, which is the one we displaced it with). So we take the
   102	upper-left
   103	envelope of the static points and report each controller's signed distance
   104	above or below it. We classify a signed distance of more than $+1.0$\,pp as
   105	above the front, less than $-1.0$\,pp as below it, and the band between the
   106	two as \emph{on} it---a tie, not a win. The band is a convention we fixed
   107	before running anything, and we report it here because every verdict in this
   108	paper depends on it. Our later replicate measurements (\S\ref{sec:results})
   109	bracket it rather than justify it: the SLO spread of configurations we ran
   110	more than once ranges from 0.5\,pp to 5.1\,pp, so for some configurations
   111	the band is generous and for others it is far too narrow. We flag this as a
   112	weakness of the criterion, not a defence of it. One further property of the
   113	envelope deserves flagging, because
   114	three of the nine controller variants finish slower than 295 minutes. Its
   115	right-hand segment runs from \texttt{cap2}+HiCache at 295 minutes to
   116	\texttt{cap1} at 527, and \texttt{cap1} is the only anchor there that runs
   117	\emph{without} HiCache. Those three---\texttt{hz2},
   118	\texttt{breshz1} and \texttt{foyfix}---are therefore judged against an
   119	interpolation between one host-tier point and one device-only point. If the
   120	envelope is drawn from the host-tier anchors alone, that segment does not exist
   121	and those three cannot be adjudicated at all; drawn from device-only anchors
   122	alone, they sit further below. We report the conservative reading and note that
   123	the honest statement about those three is \emph{below the front as we defined
   124	it}, not \emph{below every static configuration}. A fourth arm,
   125	\texttt{bres}, sat in the same position in an earlier version of this
   126	paper; its re-measured value is 265.9 minutes, which puts it back inside the
   127	device-only range, so the class has three members and not four. Two of the
   128	three
   129	(\texttt{hz2}, \texttt{breshz1}) were also measured under host
   130	co-tenancy and are marked $^\ddagger$ in Table~\ref{tab:runs}; whether they
   131	are slower than 295 minutes at all is one of the things the re-runs will
   132	settle. The front and every
   133	controller's signed distance are computed by one script from the raw per-step
   134	logs; the table generator computes the distance for the four runs that did not
   135	complete all 999 steps---both are named in \S\ref{sec:repro}---and that
   136	script's own first version was wrong: it extrapolated past the end of the
   137	envelope and declared the slowest static cap a winner. We fixed it and report
   138	the episode because it is the failure mode this criterion is most prone to.

     1	\section{Introduction}
     2	\label{sec:intro}
     3	
     4	An agentic LLM session is not a request. It is a long-lived job that issues a
     5	round of inference, waits on a tool, and comes back---tens of times---with a
     6	context that grows every round. On a memory-constrained accelerator this makes
     7	admission control look like the obvious lever. The KV pool is the binding
     8	resource; a single large session can occupy most of it; and the sessions
     9	themselves are wildly heterogeneous. If a scheduler can see how much of the
    10	pool a candidate will need and how much room is left, surely it should beat a
    11	fixed number.
    12	
    13	We believed this, built a controller that does exactly that, and spent a
    14	considerable amount of GPU time discovering that on our workload and hardware
    15	it is false. This paper reports that negative result, and the negative result is
    16	most of what we have. Alongside it we report one measurement---the elasticity of
    17	per-step cost with respect to the running count---that we hoped would say in
    18	advance how much room admission control has. It does bound that room on the
    19	configurations we measured, but when we tested whether it explains our
    20	controllers' failure, on their own configuration and on a second workload, it
    21	did not. We report it as a diagnostic of a workload, not as a predictor of
    22	whether a controller will win.
    23	
    24	\subsection{What we measured}
    25	
    26	We serve a replay of 200 real coding-agent sessions on a single RTX 3090
    27	(Qwen2.5-Coder-7B, 96K context, SGLang, a 101{,}432-token KV pool) and compare
    28	a family of admission policies against a sweep of static concurrency caps.
    29	Nine controller variants, spanning four mechanistically distinct ideas, all
    30	land below the static Pareto front, and so does a reimplementation of an
    31	independent congestion-based controller from the literature
    32	(Fig.~\ref{fig:frontier}). The best of them misses by under a point---inside the
    33	$\pm1.0$\,pp band we call a tie, but on the below side of it---and the closest
    34	arm we repeated misses by 0.9 with its repeat at 5.6.
    35	
    36	\begin{figure}[tb]
    37	  \centering
    38	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig1_frontier.pdf}
    39	  \caption{Every completed run on the (wall clock, SLO) plane. The grey line
    40	  is the upper-left envelope of the static points---drawn from the data, so it
    41	  uses each configuration's \emph{better} run where a repeat exists, which is
    42	  why \texttt{cap3}+HiCache's 236-minute run sits below the line rather than on
    43	  it; a policy below or on it has
    44	  not beaten a static cap, because time-slicing two caps approximates any
    45	  point on the segment between them. Nine \sys{} variants plus a
    46	  reimplementation of an independent controller, none above at the 10\,s
    47	  latency threshold used throughout; \S\ref{sec:discussion} reports how far
    48	  that verdict is conditional on the threshold. The five points drawn with a
    49	  hollow ring were measured while three or more other engine instances shared
    50	  the host (\S\ref{sec:repro}); on three of them the per-request decode
    51	  interval is inflated by 25--40\% and they are not comparable with the rest,
    52	  on the other two the flag is a window that left no measurable trace. The
    53	  three are being re-measured, and one arm that carried the flag in an
    54	  earlier version has been re-measured clean and is plotted without a ring.}
    55	  \label{fig:frontier}
    56	\end{figure}
    57	
    58	\subsection{Why}
    59	
    60	We decompose the wall clock into \emph{busy} time, during which the engine has
    61	at least one request in flight, and \emph{idle} time, during which it has
    62	none. The busy component is governed by $W_{\text{busy}} \approx
    63	(G/\overline{R})\,\tau$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:busy}), where $\overline{R}$ is the mean
    64	number of running requests, $G$ the generated tokens and $\tau$ the per-step
    65	cost. An admission policy moves $\overline{R}$, and on our configurations it
    66	moves little else---though \S\ref{sec:model} gives two arms where the busy and
    67	idle components move in \emph{opposite} directions, which is why the split is
    68	worth making at all.
    69	The difficulty is that $\tau$ is not constant in $\overline{R}$: across our
    70	runs $\tau_1$ spans 27.0--36.7\,ms, and on the device-only family, raising the
    71	busy-time running count from 1.00 to 1.84 absorbs a third of what the extra
    72	concurrency buys.
    73	
    74	The reason $\tau$ moves is recomputation, and whether recomputation happens is
    75	decided by the memory tier rather than by the policy. A session's prefix is
    76	worth reusing only while it is still resident, so prefix \emph{reuse} is
    77	bounded by the workload---in our trace, 75.5\% of the prompt tokens, a bound
    78	no configuration we ran reaches. How much of that bound is realized is where
    79	the tier enters. With the KV pool on the device alone, realization falls as
    80	concurrency rises---92.5\% of the bound at $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.00
    81	down to 36.4\% at 1.84---and the shortfall appears as recomputation, 8.6\,M to
    82	20.7\,M tokens. With a host tier in front of it the decline disappears: over the
    83	running counts our controllers occupy, the static points fall only from 92.4\%
    84	to 90.2\%, and the worst of the nineteen host-tier runs---the one that disables
    85	every throttle---still realizes 72.8\%, where the nearest device-only point
    86	(the cap of four, at $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.65) manages 60.7\%, while
    87	the \emph{device} misses more. Two configurations at the same running
    88	count and the same pool occupancy realize 79.0\% and 91.3\% of the bound
    89	respectively (\S\ref{sec:model}); what separates them is not how full the pool
    90	is but what happens when it overflows. That pair also differs in date and in
    91	GPU, so a capacity control that changes the pool size alone on one card is
    92	queued behind the re-runs; \S\ref{sec:results} reports it when it lands.
    93	That is the finding we would carry to another system first, and it is why this
    94	paper's answer to its own title is a property of the deployment rather than of
    95	the controller.
    96	
    97	The idle component is structural rather than a control failure. A session in a
    98	tool wait issues no request, so when all live sessions happen to be waiting,
    99	the engine is genuinely empty. The stretches are short in the middle and long
   100	in the tail---mean 20.9\,s---and the tail is what a scheduler feels: 69.9\%
   101	of all idle \emph{time} sits in stretches over 20\,s, an order of magnitude
   102	above the 2\,s control cycle. (The median is not a good summary here: it moves
   103	between 5 and 10\,s depending on which runs are pooled, because one long run
   104	contributes a fifth of the stretches.) Crucially, the tool wait itself is much longer than
   105	the trace's median suggests: the median inter-round gap is 0.35\,s, but the
   106	\emph{mean} is 15.8--16.9\,s with 22\% of gaps over ten seconds. A controller
   107	that
   108	reads only the median concludes, as we initially did, that sessions never
   109	leave and therefore that there is no idle time to recover. There is.
   110	
   111	\subsection{Contributions}
   112	
   113	\begin{itemize}
   114	  \item \textbf{We identify the memory tier as what decides how much of the
   115	        achievable prefix reuse survives added concurrency, and therefore how
   116	        much a controller has to gain by admitting more.} The two senses of
   117	        ``room'' need separating, and \S\ref{sec:xtrace} is what separates
   118	        them: the tier bounds the \emph{local} return on added concurrency
   119	        (busy-time throughput), and it does not by itself decide whether a
   120	        controller wins \emph{end to end}, where idle time and the SLO axis
   121	        also move. Our second workload is the demonstration---it has no host
   122	        tier and its controller runs at low concurrency (1.22), the two
   123	        conditions this section associates with room, and it still does not win
   124	        (\S\ref{sec:xtrace}). The claim here is the local one. The trace
   125	        bounds reuse at
   126	        75.5\% of its prompt tokens, and no configuration we ran reaches that
   127	        bound---the best realizes 92.5\% of it. What the tier decides is how
   128	        much of the bound is realized as concurrency rises: device-only,
   129	        realization falls from 92.5\% to 36.4\% between
   130	        $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.00 and 1.84 and the shortfall appears
   131	        as recomputation, 8.6\,M to 20.7\,M tokens; with a host tier it holds
   132	        at 90.2--92.4\% over 1.25 to 1.73, while the \emph{device} misses more
   133	        (\S\ref{sec:model}). Two configurations at the same running count and
   134	        the same pool occupancy realize 79.0\% and 91.3\% of the bound, so it
   135	        is not how full the pool is but what happens when it overflows; that
   136	        pair differs in date and GPU as well, so a capacity control that
   137	        varies the pool alone on one card is queued; \S\ref{sec:results}
   138	        reports it when it lands. The per-step elasticity follows the same line:
   139	        it passes one above $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}} \approx 1.5$
   140	        device-only and stays below one across the controllers' whole
   141	        operating range with a host tier (\S\ref{sec:criterion}). Two
   142	        measurements that do not share a code path---client-side step timing
   143	        and engine-side token accounting---give the same answer. This is the
   144	        finding we would carry to another system first, and it is what the
   145	        title's question turns on.
   146	  \item We test four routes by which an adaptive admission controller was
   147	        expected to beat a tuned static cap on this workload, and we are
   148	        precise about what each one rests on. Two are falsified by measurement
   149	        against criteria we fixed before the runs---changing the accounting
   150	        base (\S\ref{sec:results}) and changing the growth reserve. One is a
   151	        knob rather than a hypothesis, and we report it only because it slides
   152	        the operating point along the front and adds no mechanism. The fourth,
   153	        changing the execution granularity from session count to
   154	        running-request count, is falsified by measurement \emph{and} by an
   155	        argument about why the permit mechanism cannot express the control it
   156	        would need. We do not call the last two of these falsifications of a
   157	        stated hypothesis, because they were not.
   158	  \item We give a two-part decomposition of the wall clock (\S\ref{sec:model})
   159	        that locates the cancellation: admission policy moves the
   160	        running-request count, and the per-step cost moves with it. On the
   161	        device-only configurations we can account for that rise token for
   162	        token---evicted prefixes are recomputed almost one for one. On
   163	        host-tier configurations recomputation is flat and the account has to
   164	        come from elsewhere; we report the part of that residual we were able
   165	        to attribute, and the part we were not (\S\ref{sec:model},
   166	        \S\ref{sec:repro}).
   167	  \item We reduce the question to one measurable number, the elasticity of
   168	        per-step cost with respect to the running count (\S\ref{sec:criterion}),
   169	        and measure it at $0.38$ on the model-consistent step cost and $0.61$
   170	        on the per-request one, on the configurations whose mechanism we can
   171	        account for token for token. We offer it as a diagnostic rather than a
   172	        predictor: it says where on the curve a policy operates and how much
   173	        the remaining headroom is worth, and \S\ref{sec:criterion} is explicit
   174	        about the sense in which it is circular and the sense in which it is
   175	        not.
   176	  \item We characterize when adaptivity \emph{could} have paid, and show that
   177	        our workload is not that case: the same throttle configuration that is
   178	        worth $+51\%$ at 25 sessions costs $-42\%$ at 200 (\S\ref{sec:load}).
   179	        Put differently, no single scalar cap is optimal across both, which is
   180	        the regime adaptive control exists for. On the low-load tier, where
   181	        that argument bites hardest, the controller is \emph{not} worse---%
   182	        same-generation measurements put it on the front, $+0.9$\,pp above the
   183	        line and inside the noise. On the high-load tier it is dominated. That
   184	        asymmetry, not a blanket failure, is what this paper measures.
   185	  \item We make the measurements reproducible: the front and each controller's
   186	        signed distance are produced by one script from the raw per-step logs,
   187	        the tables and the sub-thousand-step runs by two others, all named in
   188	        \S\ref{sec:repro}. We report the six claims we had to retract along the
   189	        way, with the checks that caught them.
   190	\end{itemize}
   191	
   192	\subsection{What we do not claim}
   193	
   194	We do not claim that admission control is useless in general. We claim it does
   195	not pay \emph{here} and we show the frontier measurement that decides that; the
   196	diagnostic of \S\ref{sec:criterion} is a cheaper check an engineer can run
   197	before committing to a sweep, not a verdict on its own, and it can be taken on another
   198	workload before committing to a sweep. We also do not claim our hardware and model are
   199	representative; our results are from one 24\,GB accelerator and one 7B model,
   200	and extending them is future work.

     1	\section{Decomposing the Wall Clock}
     2	\label{sec:model}
     3	
     4	\subsection{Two components}
     5	
     6	We split the wall clock at every sampled instant according to whether the
     7	engine has any request in flight:
     8	\begin{equation}
     9	  W \;=\; W_{\text{busy}} \;+\; W_{\text{idle}} .
    10	  \label{eq:split}
    11	\end{equation}
    12	Fig.~\ref{fig:decomp} shows the split for the twenty-eight runs of
    13	Table~\ref{tab:runs}'s families that we have per-step logs for. Two things are
    14	immediately visible and both matter later.
    15	
    16	First, idle time is not noise. Across the runs it spans 7.3\% to 41.9\% of
    17	the sampled span---7.3\% for the fastest configuration, 36.3\% for the
    18	slowest, and 41.9\% for the one that idles most (all-throttles-off). The
    19	denominator here
    20	is the span the sampler observed, which runs about six minutes longer than the
    21	request wall clock of Table~\ref{tab:runs}, so the fractions against that
    22	wall clock are 7.6\% and 36.7\%---and it is not spread evenly.
    23	Over 1{,}844 stretches the mean is 20.9\,s and 17.5\% of them exceed
    24	20\,s, accounting for 69.9\% of all idle \emph{time}
    25	(Fig.~\ref{fig:idle}). The tail, not the middle, is what a scheduler feels,
    26	and it sits at the scale of the 15.8--16.9\,s mean tool wait (demanded and
    27	measured; \texttt{toolwait.csv}). We report the mean and the share rather
    28	than the median here because the median is not robust to which runs enter the
    29	pool: it is 10.0\,s without \texttt{cap1} and 5.0--5.5\,s with it, since
    30	that one run contributes 411 of the 1{,}844 stretches and is itself the
    31	longest. The mean (18.8--20.9\,s) and the over-20\,s share of idle time
    32	(64.6--70.1\%) move by little across the four poolings we report in
    33	\texttt{idle\_pooled.csv}, so the claim that the tail dominates does not
    34	depend on the choice. The 90th percentile moves with the median
    35	(25.1--30.1\,s) and we give it as a range for the same reason.
    36	
    37	Second, the two components move independently, and which way idle moves tells
    38	you nothing about which way the wall clock moves. The no-control baseline
    39	(\S\ref{sec:results}) cuts idle time by 13.8 minutes against the base
    40	configuration---it admits everything, so nothing queues---and finishes only
    41	2.7 minutes sooner, because its busy time grows by 11.1 minutes; the trade
    42	costs it the SLO column outright, 89.8\% down to 3.0\%. The
    43	no-gate-no-reservation arm moves the other way: idle \emph{rises} by 10.1
    44	minutes while busy falls by 29.2, for a 19.0-minute gain. Any account that
    45	treats ``more concurrency'' as one thing will get this wrong in one direction
    46	or the other. Neither example is one of the damaged runs of
    47	\S\ref{sec:repro}: the baseline is clean and the no-gate arm carries the
    48	weaker \emph{watch} flag, three lanes for over an hour with no measured
    49	inflation. The charge-free base arm is a third case, and we give its split in
    50	\S\ref{sec:results} with the re-measured numbers.
    51

    53	  \centering

    55	  \caption{Wall clock split into busy and idle for the twenty-eight runs of
    56	  \S\ref{sec:model} and \S\ref{sec:results}, ordered by total duration. Idle is 7.3\% for the fastest configuration and
    57	  36.3\% for the slowest. Note the no-control baseline: it sits sixth of
    58	  twenty-eight on idle share (11.4\%) and yet sixteenth on duration, because
    59	  its busy time grows with everything it admits. Five bars are
    60	  hatched---their busy time is inflated by host
    61	  co-tenancy (\S\ref{sec:repro}); the hatch is the same $^\ddagger$ flag as in

    67	  \centering
    68	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig4_idle_segments.pdf}
    69	  \caption{Survival function of idle-stretch duration, pooled over all
    70	  completed runs (1{,}844 stretches). Most stretches are short---the median is
    71	  5--10\,s depending on which runs enter the pool, which is why we do not lead
    72	  with it---but the minutes are not: 69.9\% of all idle \emph{time} sits in

    78	
    79	\subsection{What determines busy time}
    80	
    81	During busy time the engine executes steps; each step produces one token per
    82	running request. Hence
    83	\begin{equation}
    84	  W_{\text{busy}} \;\approx\; \frac{G}{\overline{R}}\,\tau ,
    85	  \label{eq:busy}
    86	\end{equation}
    87	with $G$ the total generated tokens (fixed by the trace at 706{,}010) and
    88	$\tau$ the per-step cost. Across our runs $\tau$ is not a fit parameter we
    89	tuned; we obtain it two independent ways, and reporting both is what shows
    90	they are measuring different things rather than confirming each other.
    91	
    92	\paragraph{Method 1 (derived).} $\tau_1 = W_{\text{busy}} \cdot \overline{R} /
    93	G$. Two choices in that expression are easy to get wrong and both matter. The
    94	time term must be the \emph{busy} component, not the wall clock---using the
    95	wall clock charges idle time to the steps. And $\overline{R}$ must be the mean
    96	running count \emph{during busy time}, not the mean over the whole run: the
    97	whole-run mean includes the idle samples, where the count is zero, so it equals
    98	$\overline{R}_{\text{busy}} \times$ (busy fraction). Using it makes $\tau_1$
    99	too low by exactly the busy fraction---so the estimate is short by the
   100	\emph{idle} fraction, 36.3\% for \texttt{cap1}, whose busy share is 63.7\%. We report both columns in the data, and use the
   101	busy-conditional mean throughout; the whole-run mean is what an earlier
   102	revision of this paper used, and it made the two methods agree far better than
   103	they do.
   104	
   105	\paragraph{Method 2 (independent).} For each step record we compute the
   106	per-request decode interval,
   107	$(\text{total\_duration} - \text{first\_token}) / (\text{output\_len} - 1)$,
   108	and take the median over steps with $\text{output\_len} \ge 32$.
   109	
   110	The two do not agree exactly, and the disagreement is informative rather than
   111	embarrassing. $\tau_1$ exceeds $\tau_2$ on every run we accept, by 7.7--39.7\%
   112	(\texttt{decomp.csv}, column \texttt{excess\_pct}), which in
   113	absolute terms is 2.6--8.2\,ms of a 27--37\,ms step. (The one run we exclude
   114	from the reuse accounting, the \texttt{horizon}=1 repeat, reads 66.3\% here
   115	against its original's 18.5\%---a second engine-side counter that is out of
   116	line with its own configuration, which is why we treat that run's numbers as
   117	unusable rather than as a measurement we dislike.) The gap is
   118	\emph{consistent with} engine steps that are prefill and
   119	therefore emit no output token---we can bound it, not identify it, because
   120	subtracting a median from a mean does not isolate a component. All we can form
   121	from these two is their ratio: the excess $\tau_1/\tau_2 - 1$ runs
   122	17.7--39.7\% across the five device-only configurations. Two things follow, and
   123	we separate them. First, it is a ratio of \emph{times}, not of steps, and we
   124	have no measurement of the fraction of step \emph{counts} that are prefill---
   125	chunked prefill makes those two different numbers. Second, even as a time
   126	ratio it is the gap between two measurements, not a decomposition of one: it
   127	is consistent with prefill and non-decode work filling it, but we cannot rule
   128	out that some of the difference is the two estimators disagreeing for other
   129	reasons. So the two are not
   130	two estimates of one number; they are measurements of two different things,
   131	and we do not size a component with their difference. Fig.~\ref{fig:steptime} shows both, run by run. Method 2 remains the one that does not depend on the
   132	quantity it is used to explain, and it is the one we quote when the question
   133	is the cost of a decode step. The engine's own latency counters
   134	(\texttt{itl\_p50}, \texttt{e2e\_p50}, \texttt{ttft\_p50}) are exported as zero
   135	by SGLang v0.5.10 in our configuration, so no directly measured step time
   136	exists in our data; instrumenting this is future work.
   137	
   138	\begin{figure}[tb]
   139	  \centering
   140	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig3_steptime.pdf}
   141	  \caption{Per-step cost by two independent measurements, one run per row:
   142	  circles are model-consistent (all engine steps), crosses are the per-request
   143	  decode interval. They do not agree---$\tau_1$ exceeds $\tau_2$ by 8--40\%---
   144	  and the gap is consistent with the prefill share, not measurement error;
   145	  \S\ref{sec:model} bounds that share rather than identifying it. The dotted line is
   146	  the cost of reading the weights once; every run sits above it. The six rows
   147	  marked $^\ddagger$ were measured under host co-tenancy, which is why they
   148	  cluster at the high end---their step times are the inflated ones
   149	  (\S\ref{sec:repro}).}
   150	  \label{fig:steptime}
   151	\end{figure}
   152	
   153	\subsection{The per-step cost is not constant in concurrency}
   154	
   155	Equation~\ref{eq:busy} would be uninteresting if $\tau$ were a constant. It is
   156	not. Across the thirteen runs we fitted---the five device-only caps, the three
   157	host-tier caps that span the same range, and the five controller arms we report
   158	individually---$\tau_1$ ranges from 27.0 to 36.7\,ms and $\tau_2$
   159	from 20.6 to 33.1\,ms, and both rise with the running count: fitting
   160	Eq.~\ref{eq:elasticity} to the five device-only configurations gives
   161	$\varepsilon = 0.38$ for $\tau_1$ and $0.61$ for $\tau_2$
   162	($R^2 = 0.89$ for the latter, Fig.~\ref{fig:cancel}).
   163	
   164	The relation is a trend, not a law. Two host-tier configurations with similar
   165	running counts behave differently: \texttt{cap4\_hc} runs 1.73 requests at
   166	29.8\,ms while \texttt{F:hz0} runs 1.66 at 35.7\,ms. Running count sets a
   167	floor and a trend, not a value---and on the host-tier family, as we show below,
   168	it does not set the value at all.
   169	
   170	This is the crux of the paper, and we state it at the strength the arithmetic
   171	supports. Hold $\tau_1$ at its value for \texttt{cap1}, 28.8\,ms, and busy
   172	time falls as $1/\overline{R}$: raising the running count from 1.00 to 1.84
   173	should have cut busy time by a factor of 1.84, from 340 minutes to 184. The
   174	measured curve flattens and lands at 234. The rise in $\tau$ takes back 50 of
   175	the 156 minutes that the extra concurrency would otherwise have bought---\emph{a
   176	third} of the gain, not all of it and, as an earlier revision of this paper
   177	claimed, not more than half. The other two thirds are real: raising
   178	concurrency does make the engine faster. What it does not do is make it faster
   179	in proportion, and the shortfall compounds with every controller that tries to
   180	exploit the difference.
   181	
   182	\paragraph{What the engine is doing when it is not decoding.} SGLang exposes
   183	two counters that answer this directly: tokens evicted from the radix cache,
   184	and prompt tokens that had to be prefilled rather than reused. Across the five
   185	static caps \emph{without} HiCache the two track each other almost exactly.
   186	\texttt{cap1} evicts 9.2\,M tokens and prefills 8.6\,M uncached;
   187	\texttt{cap5} evicts 21.3\,M and prefills 20.7\,M; the gap is 0.6--0.7\,M on
   188	every one of the five. An evicted prefix is recomputed, close to one token for
   189	one, and that recomputation is chunked into decode steps. Those same five runs
   190	give recomputation rising with $\tau$ across four of the five points: 9.2\,M
   191	uncached tokens at 27.4\,ms, 11.5 at 29.7, 15.4 at 32.4, 20.7 at 36.7. The
   192	exception is the first point, \texttt{cap1}, which recomputes least (8.6\,M)
   193	and still costs 28.8\,ms---more than \texttt{cap2} does for more recomputation.
   194	We report the ordering we have rather than rounding it to a monotone one: it is
   195	a mechanism consistent with the $\tau$ trend on four of the five device-only
   196	configurations, and one point it does not order. It is five points on five
   197	different policies, not an
   198	intervention: we did not hold concurrency fixed and vary eviction, so we call
   199	it consistent rather than demonstrated.
   200	
   201	\begin{figure}[tb]
   202	  \centering
   203	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig7_elasticity.pdf}
   204	  \caption{Per-step cost against the running count during busy time. Solid
   205	  circles are $\tau_1$ and squares $\tau_2$ for the five device-only
   206	  configurations; $\tau_1$ is model-consistent (it averages over all engine
   207	  steps, including prefill that emits no output token), $\tau_2$ is the
   208	  per-request decode interval, and $\tau_1$ exceeds $\tau_2$ by 8--40\%. The
   209	  green triangles are $\tau_1$ for the three HiCache configurations---the ones
   210	  the controllers run---and they are drawn against the same axes to make
   211	  \S\ref{sec:criterion}'s point visible: over $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.25
   212	  to 1.73, which covers the whole controller operating range, the host-tier
   213	  curve rises 27.0 to 29.8\,ms while the device-only curve over a comparable
   214	  span rises 27.4 to 36.7. We plot $\tau_1$ only for the HiCache points to keep
   215	  the figure readable. The fitted exponent is the elasticity $\varepsilon$ of
   216	  Eq.~\ref{eq:elasticity}; it is fit to the device-only points alone.}
   217	  \label{fig:cancel}
   218	\end{figure}
   219	
   220	\paragraph{Where the mechanism stops.} With HiCache enabled the
   221	correspondence breaks---and it breaks in a way that confirms the mechanism
   222	rather than undermining it. The device cache misses as much as before:
   223	\texttt{cap4\_hc} misses 22.4\,M tokens on the device, more than the
   224	device-only \texttt{cap4} misses. But only 9.1\,M of that is recomputed; the
   225	other 13.3\,M is served from the host tier. Across the nineteen host-tier
   226	runs the host serves 9.5--19.3\,M tokens each, and across the five device-only
   227	runs it serves almost nothing---0.006\% to 0.25\% of the device miss, the rest
   228	of which is recomputed on all five. Recomputation is still what the device cannot
   229	absorb; the host tier is a second absorber in front of it.
   230	
   231	\subsection{The tier, not the policy, decides whether reuse survives}
   232	
   233	The device-miss column hides the quantity that matters. Define
   234	\emph{reuse} as the prompt tokens served from anywhere in the memory
   235	hierarchy---device radix cache plus host tier---so that
   236	$\text{reuse} + \text{recomputed} = \text{prompt}$, with the prompt fixed at
   237	28.5\,M by the trace.
   238	
   239	The bound on reuse has to be defined independently of what we observed,
   240	or the argument is circular. We take it from the trace: summing the planned
   241	reusable prefix (\texttt{prefix\_len}) over all 999 rounds of
   242	\texttt{replay\_pois200\_l04.csv} gives 21{,}540{,}371 tokens against a prompt
   243	total of 28{,}526{,}871, so no policy can reuse more than 75.5\% of the
   244	prompt. That is a property of the workload. It is also a bound rather than a
   245	description: \emph{no configuration we ran reaches it}, and the best realizes
   246	92.5\%, so the gap between ``planned'' and ``achieved'' exists even in the
   247	easiest case. What varies across configurations is how much of the bound is
   248	realized, and that is where the tier enters.
   249	
   250	\begin{center}
   251	% 这张表在 conference 模板下比单栏宽 56.9pt（Overfull 会一直挂在编译日志里）。
   252	% \small + tabcolsep 3pt 后 244.4pt < 252pt。改列宽不是改数字。
   253	\small\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}%
   254	\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}
   255	\toprule
   256	device-only & $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ & 1.00 & 1.27 & 1.53 & 1.65 & 1.84 \\
   257	            & realized (\% of bound) & 92.5 & 89.7 & 79.0 & 60.7 & 36.4 \\
   258	            & recomputed (M)         &  8.6 &  9.2 & 11.5 & 15.4 & 20.7 \\
   259	\midrule
   260	host tier   & $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ & 1.25 & 1.51 & 1.73 & & \\
   261	            & realized (\% of bound) & 92.4 & 91.3 & 90.2 & & \\
   262	            & recomputed (M)         &  8.6 &  8.9 &  9.1 & & \\
   263	\bottomrule
   264	\end{tabular}
   265	\end{center}
   266	
   267	Device-only, realization falls from 92.5\% to 36.4\% and the shortfall is
   268	recomputation: 8.6\,M to 20.7\,M tokens. With a host tier it holds at
   269	90.2--92.4\% on the static points that span the same range, and at
   270	72.8--92.5\% across the nineteen host-tier runs that completed this
   271	trace---the same trace, HiCache on, at least 995 of its 999 steps---including
   272	every controller arm; the lowest is the arm that disables every throttle and
   273	therefore evicts the most (21.1\,M tokens, the largest in the table). That rule
   274	on its own selects \emph{twenty}, and we name the four runs it is not meant to
   275	catch, because a reader scanning the logs will find them: two that stopped at
   276	763 and 770 steps (they realize 70.7\% and 67.1\%, and we exclude them for not
   277	finishing rather than for their values), the variable-load family (a different
   278	trace, and short of the step count as well), and one whose engine
   279	prompt-token counter reads $1.41\times$ the trace plan with zero retractions
   280	and no client-side retries, which we could not reconcile.
   281	while the \emph{device} misses more than the device-only family's at every
   282	point (21.6--27.9\,M against 8.6--20.7\,M), because the host serves
   283	9.5--19.3\,M in front of the recomputation instead of letting the device keep
   284	it. A reader watching only the device hit rate would conclude the host tier
   285	made things worse. (Two denominators appear here and we name them: the 75.5\%
   286	is the trace's bound as a share of the prompt, and 92.5\% is the best observed
   287	reuse as a share of \emph{that bound}---\texttt{cap1}, the run that realizes
   288	it, reuses 69.9\% of its prompt. \texttt{reuse.csv} carries \texttt{reuse\_frac} and
   289	\texttt{realized\_frac} side by side so the two cannot be confused.)
   290	
   291	This is the same collapse \S\ref{sec:related} reports under a different
   292	normalization. That section quotes the round-1-and-later prefix hit rate---%
   293	steps restricted to rounds $\ge 1$, unweighted mean over steps---which falls
   294	from 80.8\% to 32.8\% across the device-only caps and holds at 80.7\% to
   295	78.7\% with a host tier. The two conventions differ in two ways, not one:
   296	which steps are in the set (round $\ge 1$ against all rounds) and how steps
   297	are combined (unweighted mean against token-weighted sum). We use the
   298	token-weighted all-steps convention here because reuse and recomputation must
   299	add to the prompt, and we name the other whenever we quote it. Both are
   300	\emph{realized} quantities that vary with the policy; neither is the trace's
   301	planned rate, which is fixed at 75.5\% and appears in this section only as
   302	the bound.
   303	
   304	Device occupancy does not explain the split. \texttt{cap3\_fixed} and
   305	\texttt{cap3\_hc} run at nearly the same mean device occupancy (55.8\% and
   306	55.5\%) and nearly the same running count (1.53 and 1.51), and they realize
   307	79.0\% and 91.3\% of the bound respectively. What differs is not how full the
   308	device pool is but what happens when it overflows: with no host tier an
   309	evicted prefix is gone and must be recomputed; with one it is demoted and read
   310	back. The pool is the binding resource, the tier sets the pool, and the tier
   311	therefore sets whether added concurrency is paid for in recomputation. The
   312	caveat is that this pair is two runs on two different days and two different
   313	GPUs, not a paired experiment; \S\ref{sec:repro} states what we did to rule
   314	out co-tenancy. A capacity-controlled version---same card, same trace, only
   315	the pool size changed---is queued behind the re-runs and we report it in
   316	\S\ref{sec:results} when it lands; we do not use it here.
   317	
   318	\paragraph{The second trace agrees on direction and not on level.}
   319	We ran the same accounting on a second workload, reported in full in
   320	\S\ref{sec:xtrace}---117 real Claude agent sessions, 390 rounds, the same pool
   321	and the same engine---and it reproduces the direction: its realization of \emph{its own} bound falls from 74.8\% to 57.5\%
   322	as the running count rises from 1.08 to 1.50, and over that span the whole-range
   323	per-unit slope is $-40.9$ points, against the device-only family's $-66.8$ over
   324	$\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.00--1.84 (\texttt{slope.csv}). What it does not reproduce is the level, and this is
   325	worth stating because it bounds what we can claim. The lightest cap on that
   326	trace (\texttt{realhr\_cap2}) runs at a \emph{lower} median device occupancy
   327	than the lightest on this one (15.8\% against 22.4\%, \texttt{cap1}) and
   328	realizes \emph{less} (74.8\% against 92.5\%), so realization is not a function
   329	of occupancy alone---and a
   330	statement of the form ``the pool collapses above $x$\% occupancy'' is wrong on
   331	our own data. Occupancy is instantaneous while reuse depends on history; a
   332	session's prefix must survive from one round to the next, and how much
   333	pressure that puts on the pool is not visible in what the pool holds at an
   334	instant. We do not have the measurement that would separate those, which is
   335	why the controlled experiment we report in \S\ref{sec:results} varies the
   336	pool alone, on one card and one trace, rather than leaning on the cross-trace
   337	comparison. The claim this section supports is the narrow one: within a
   338	configuration, the host tier preserves realization at a matched running count
   339	and a matched occupancy, and without it realization falls.
   340	
   341	That gives the criterion of \S\ref{sec:criterion} a mechanism rather than a
   342	correlation, and it suggests a prediction we are testing:
   343	if the tier acts by multiplying usable capacity (the host pool is twice the
   344	device pool, so three times in total), then shrinking the device pool alone
   345	should move the collapse point even with no host tier involved. We do not
   346	treat the arithmetic as a result---it assumes KV demand grows linearly in the
   347	running count, which heterogeneous sessions need not satisfy---and the
   348	queued experiment measures the collapse point directly rather than predicting
   349	it, and \S\ref{sec:results} reports it once it has run.
   350	
   351	\paragraph{What is left unexplained.} Less than an earlier draft of this
   352	paragraph claimed. Across the nineteen host-tier runs recomputation spans
   353	8.6--12.8\,M tokens and realization 72.8--92.5\%, and $\tau_1$ spans
   354	27.0--36.2\,ms. Three of those runs
   355	(\texttt{F:hz2} 33.6, \texttt{F:hz0} 35.7,
   356	\texttt{F:breshz1} 36.2\,ms) were
   357	measured while three other engine instances shared the host, which inflates
   358	per-step cost by 25--40\% and leaves the volume columns untouched
   359	(\S\ref{sec:repro}); they are being re-measured and we do not use their
   360	timing. A fourth was re-measured clean and is in the set above with its new
   361	value. Across the remaining sixteen the $\tau_1$ spread does not sit
   362	unexplained at all: it tracks their recomputation closely (a straight line
   363	through the sixteen leaves every run within $1.5$\,ms, $r = +0.92$), which is
   364	the mechanism of this section holding inside the host tier rather than a
   365	residual. We report the association and not the line: the coefficients are
   366	fitted to one configuration family and we have no derivation for them. We note that the negative
   367	result does not rest on it either way: the cancellation is measured directly,
   368	not inferred from the mechanism.
   369	
   370	One caution about the strength of the account above, since the arithmetic
   371	invites overreading. $\text{reuse} + \text{recomputed} = \text{prompt}$ is a
   372	definition, not a finding, so ``the shortfall reappears as recomputation''
   373	follows from how we partitioned the prompt and cannot by itself establish the
   374	mechanism. What is independent of the partition is the pair of engine
   375	counters: evicted tokens and uncached prefill tokens are separate SGLang
   376	counters, and on the device-only runs they track within 0.6--0.7\,M on all
   377	five---which is the observation that licenses reading eviction as the cause
   378	of recomputation. With a host tier the two come apart by design, and the
   379	1.3--8.6\,M gap between them is an upper bound on what the host absorbed out
   380	of the evicted pool: those tokens were evicted and not recomputed, which is
   381	consistent with the host having served them and equally consistent with their
   382	never having been asked for again. We can bound it, not identify it
   383	(\texttt{eviction.csv}); the host serves 9.5--19.3\,M in total, so most of what
   384	it serves are prefixes that were still resident on the device when the
   385	session came back for them.
   386	
   387	\paragraph{A refutation we had to withdraw.} An earlier revision withdrew this
   388	mechanism outright, on two checks that were both wrong. The first was that
   389	total prefilled tokens equalled the trace's planned prefill to a ratio of 1.00,
   390	so no token could have been computed twice. \texttt{prompt\_tokens\_total}
   391	counts the full submitted prompt \emph{including} its cached portion, so it
   392	equals the sum of prompt lengths by construction and cannot detect
   393	recomputation; the quantity that can is the uncached count used above, which
   394	varies by 2.4$\times$ across these runs. The second was that SGLang reported
   395	no retractions. Radix-cache eviction is not a retraction---retractions count
   396	running requests preempted mid-flight, a different event with its own counter.
   397	We record the episode because it is the same failure mode as the segment
   398	argument of \S\ref{sec:bg}: a check that looks like a measurement but is an
   399	identity.
   400	
   401	\subsection{Why idle time exists, and why recovering it does not pay}
   402	
   403	The length of an individual idle stretch is a property of the workload, not of
   404	the controller. A session in a
   405	tool wait holds its permit and its KV but issues no request; with only about
   406	two live sessions, the probability that all of them are simultaneously waiting
   407	is substantial. The direct evidence is the duration of the stretches
   408	themselves: they are of the same order as the tool waits, and an order of
   409	magnitude longer than the 2\,s control cycle (Fig.~\ref{fig:idle}). No
   410	controller can shorten a gap that the tool set.
   411	
   412	How much idle a \emph{run} accumulates is a different matter, and there the
   413	controller is not off the hook. Idle accumulates when every live session
   414	happens to be parked at once, and how many sessions are live is precisely what
   415	an admission policy sets. Two configurations with the same mean running count
   416	make this visible: \texttt{horizon}=1 (1.52) and \texttt{cap3}+HiCache (1.51)
   417	idle 36.7 and 24.1 minutes respectively---a 52\% difference at matched
   418	concurrency---and 12.6 of the 19.4-minute wall-clock gap between them is that
   419	idle difference rather than step cost. So the gap durations are the workload's,
   420	and the number of gaps is partly the policy's.
   421	
   422	We note one claim we had to drop here. An earlier draft reported that idle
   423	fraction falls monotonically with mean concurrency. With the full data it does
   424	not: on the busy-conditional measure this section uses throughout,
   425	\texttt{F:hz0} at $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.67 idles 15.2\% while
   426	\texttt{cap4} at 1.65 idles 10.4\%---more concurrency and \emph{more} idle,
   427	the reverse of the reported trend. (The two arms read 1.41 and 1.48 on the
   428	all-samples mean, where the ordering happens to run the other way;
   429	\texttt{arm\_running.csv} carries both conventions side by side, and we name
   430	the one each claim uses.) The weaker statement that survives is the one we use---%
   431	the stretches are structural and long.
   432	
   433	The obvious remedy---keep more sessions live so that someone is always
   434	ready---is what \texttt{bres} does. It works in the narrow sense: \texttt{bres}
   435	recovers about 17 of the minutes that its own baseline spends idle. It is
   436	still slower overall, because the sessions it keeps live raise the step cost
   437	by more than the recovered idle is worth---and, as above, that step-cost rise
   438	is measured under co-tenancy and is therefore an upper bound. Idle is not unrecoverable; it is
   439	recoverable at a price that is not worth paying here.
   440	The structural remedy would be to make the engine do work during a wait, but
   441	there is no work to do: the session's next round does not exist until its tool
   442	returns.

 succeeded in 9ms:
     1	\section{Results: Four Routes, One Outcome}
     2	\label{sec:results}
     3	
     4	\subsection{The frontier}
     5	
     6	Fig.~\ref{fig:frontier} places every completed run on the (wall clock, SLO)
     7	plane and draws the upper-left envelope of the static points. Table~\ref{tab:runs}
     8	gives the numbers and each controller's signed distance from that envelope.
     9	
    10	The result is unambiguous. Of nine controller variants, \emph{none} is above
    11	the envelope---not one, on any run, and the pair we repeated most tightly says
    12	so at a resolution the others do not. The closest arm of all is the one with
    13	single-flight admission disabled, at $0.55$ percentage points \emph{below} the
    14	line---inside our $\pm1.0$\,pp tie band, so we record it as \emph{on} the
    15	line rather than below it, but on the below side of it.
    16	
    17	The base configuration is the one whose repeats we can trust, and they are
    18	tight: four runs spanning 0.5\,pp of SLO (89.5\% mean; 279.8, 289.6, 291.3
    19	and 293.2 minutes at 89.5\%, 89.3\%, 89.8\% and 89.6\%---the matched-pair
    20	experiment ran the same configuration twice more, as \texttt{pairA\_foyerhc}
    21	and \texttt{pairB\_foyerhc}), against the 3.3\,pp that separates it from the
    22	single-flight-off arm. So that
    23	difference is several times the noise of its own configuration rather than
    24	inside it, and it is a trade rather than a win: 28 minutes of wall clock bought
    25	with 3.5\,pp of SLO, moving the arm onto the tie band from below it. Not one
    26	configuration in the table gets above the line, and the tightest pair we have
    27	is what makes us willing to say the near misses are misses. A fifth pair, two runs of \texttt{cap3}+HiCache, \emph{is} used, and an
    28	earlier draft of this paper wrongly set it aside saying one of them exceeded
    29	its own cap. That was a different run: the over-cap one is the device-only
    30	\texttt{cap3} repeat (peak in-flight five against a cap of three; at our
    31	300-point in-flight sample grid, 126 samples sit over the cap, a count that
    32	scales with the grid rather than with the run), while both HiCache runs peak at three with none. The
    33	repeat is also both faster and higher on SLO (230.3 minutes, 81.9\% against
    34	236.4 and 81.1\%), so by the same rule that displaced \texttt{cap4}+HiCache's
    35	first run it displaces this one---and moving that anchor is what puts our
    36	best arm below the line rather than on it.\footnote{The repeats are uneven, and
    37	we do not want the tightest pair to stand for all of them. Five configurations
    38	have been run more than once; their SLO spreads are 0.5, 0.8, 1.0, 3.9 and
    39	5.1\,pp (\texttt{replicates.csv}). The two widest belong to the two arms that
    40	decide something---\texttt{horizon}=1 and the leftmost anchor---so where a small
    41	margin matters our repeats are weakest, and where they are strongest (the base
    42	configuration and the \texttt{cap3}+HiCache anchor) the margin is not small. We
    43	report both ends rather than the average.}
    44	
    45	The anchor repeats moved the frontier twice and we report both moves. The
    46	leftmost anchor's second run came out both faster (209.1 against 213.0 minutes)
    47	and higher on SLO (68.5 against 63.4), displacing the first; no controller
    48	verdict moves, because every controller finishes to the right of the next
    49	anchor. The middle anchor's repeat moved it the other way for us: it is also
    50	faster and higher, and taking it moves our best arm from \emph{on} the line to
    51	\emph{on the line, below it}. We report every repeat we ran rather than keeping
    52	the better of each pair, because a frontier whose anchors are single
    53	measurements is the weakness this whole subsection is about---and here it is
    54	the weakness biting.
    55	
    56	The other end of the front deserves its own sentence, because the rest of this
    57	paper is negative and a reader could close it thinking the answer is to do
    58	nothing. The run with \emph{no} admission control---every session admitted the
    59	moment it arrives---finishes in 288.6 minutes at 3.0\% SLO, $87.6$\,pp below
    60	the envelope and the worst SLO we report. Its median
    61	time-to-first-token is 1{,}774 seconds, its prefix cache hit rate collapses from
    62	the planned 75.5\% the runner prints in its end-of-run summary (the all-steps
    63	convention, a different
    64	one from the round-1+ numbers in \S\ref{sec:related}---for the same trace
    65	\texttt{gen\_nocontrol.py} reports 73.8\% on that convention) to 1.6\%, and its segment SLO is 30\% in the first
    66	tenth of the run and exactly zero in each of the nine after that. Uncontrolled
    67	admission does not degrade gracefully; it destroys the cache and then serves
    68	almost nobody. The question this paper addresses is therefore not whether to
    69	admit selectively, but which selective rule to use.
    70	
    71	Two features of the front are worth noting before the negative results.
    72	The envelope is steep between 209 and 230 minutes (0.63\,pp per minute) and
    73	shallow thereafter (0.15\,pp per minute), so the cost of buying SLO by
    74	retreating from the fast end is high. And $W$ is not monotone in concurrency:
    75	five sessions (260.9\,min) is \emph{slower} than four (249.9\,min)---the same
    76	configuration family, no host cache on either side---because the per-step cost
    77	rises faster than the request count does. We return to this
    78	non-monotonicity in \S\ref{sec:model}, where it turns out to be the first
    79	appearance of the mechanism that drives the whole frontier.
    80

   148	    horizon$=$3 (2nd run) & 293.2 & 2.07 & 89.6 & $-$1.73 \\
   149	    horizon$=$2$^\ddagger$ & 306.1 & 2.22 & 86.9 & $-$4.81 \\
   150	    horizon$=$1 (repeat) & 260.8 & 2.69 & 80.9 & $-$5.58 \\
   151	    resident base      & 265.9 & 2.41 & 85.9 & $-$1.33 \\
   152	    horizon$=$0$^\ddagger$ & 290.0 & 2.69 & 77.2 & $-$13.60 \\
   153	    horizon$=$1, no hw$^\ddagger$ & 310.9 & 2.77 & 72.3 & $-$19.54 \\
   154	    no gate, no resv. & 272.3 & 3.25 & 66.6 & $-$21.62 \\
   155	    running gate$=$4  & 282.3 & 3.03 & 64.3 & $-$25.39 \\
   156	    all throttles off & 415.0 & 2.58 & 46.1 & $-$47.10 \\
   157	    \midrule
   158	    \multicolumn{5}{l}{\emph{Independent adaptive controller (\S\ref{sec:results:aimd})}}\\
   159	    AIMD, paper params$^\ddagger$ & 364.3 & 1.91 & 81.4 & $-$11.16 \\
   160	    AIMD, retuned     & 264.6 & 3.87 & 52.8 & $-$34.28 \\
