     1	\section{Load Dependence}
     2	\label{sec:load}
     3	
     4	A negative result about adaptivity invites the obvious objection: adaptivity
     5	pays when conditions \emph{change}, and a single stationary trace is the one
     6	case where a static cap should be near-optimal. We agree. What we test here is
     7	the weaker thing---two separate constant-load tiers---and we also ran the
     8	stronger one, a trace whose load moves mid-run, whose baselines co-tenancy made
     9	unusable; \S\ref{sec:discussion} reports both what this section leaves open and
    10	why that experiment has no result to give.
    11	
    12	We run the same controller and the same cap family on a 25-session tier. The
    13	throttle configuration is reversed: with all safety throttles disabled, the
    14	25-session tier runs \emph{51\% faster} (58.4 to 28.8 minutes) at a cost of
    15	only 2.4\,pp of SLO, while on the 200-session tier disabling the same
    16	throttles costs 42\% (291.3 to 415.0 minutes) and 43.7\,pp of SLO. One switch,
    17	two regimes, opposite signs, both around 40--50\%
    18	(Fig.~\ref{fig:loadtier}). No single scalar setting is optimal across both.
    19	
    20	We flag the generation gap rather than let the figure imply more than we ran.
    21	The two 200-session numbers are current; the two 25-session numbers are from an
    22	earlier harness revision, and we never ran the all-throttles-off arm on the
    23	25-session tier under the current one. So the reversal is a direction we
    24	measured across two harness generations, not a same-generation paired
    25	comparison, and a reviewer should read it that way. The same-generation
    26	25-session measurement we do have is in Table~\ref{tab:tier25}, and it does not
    27	contain that arm.
    28	
    29	This is the regime in which adaptive control should win, and we now have the
    30	same-generation measurement that earlier drafts of this paper were missing.
    31	Four arms on the 25-session tier, all under the current harness
    32	(Table~\ref{tab:tier25}): static caps of two, three and four finish in 32.4,
    33	24.2 and 23.6 minutes at 92.8\%, 91.2\% and 79.2\% SLO, and \sys{} finishes
    34	in 27.6 minutes at 92.8\%.
    35	
    36	\begin{table}[tb]
    37	  \caption{The 25-session tier, all four arms under the current harness.
    38	  This is the same-generation comparison earlier drafts of this paper were
    39	  missing; \texttt{cap4} and the \sys{} arm were run for this revision. The
    40	  segment SD here is over six segments, not the ten Table~\ref{tab:runs} uses
    41	  for 999-step runs, so the two tables' SD columns are not comparable.}
    42	  \label{tab:tier25}
    43	  \centering
    44	  \small
    45	  \setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
    46	  \begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
    47	    \toprule
    48	    Policy & Wall (min) & Conc. & SLO (\%) & Seg.\ SD (pp) \\
    49	    \midrule
    50	    cap2          & 32.4 & 1.95 & 92.8 & 11.0 \\
    51	    cap3          & 24.2 & 2.77 & 91.2 &  5.1 \\
    52	    cap4          & 23.6 & 3.39 & 79.2 & 21.4 \\
    53	    \sys{}        & 27.6 & 2.47 & 92.8 &  7.1 \\
    54	    \bottomrule
    55	  \end{tabular}
    56	\end{table}
    57	
    58	The finding is a trade, not a defeat, and it is the opposite of what the
    59	200-session tier shows. Against the same-generation envelope---which runs from
    60	\texttt{cap4} at (23.6, 79.2) through \texttt{cap3} at (24.2, 91.2) to
    61	\texttt{cap2} at (32.4, 92.8)---\sys{} sits $+0.9$\,pp above the line. A
    62	static cap of three is still 12\% faster in wall clock, but it gives up
    63	1.6\,pp of SLO to do it, so neither dominates the other. On this tier the
    64	controller is a different point on the front rather than a dominated one,
    65	which is exactly what the 200-session tier denies it.
    66	
    67	We are careful about the size, and about the yardstick. $+0.9$\,pp is
    68	measured against a straight-line interpolation between static points, not
    69	against a switching policy anyone ran, so the defensible statement is that
    70	\sys{} is not dominated by any static point we measured on this tier---not
    71	that it beats what a static mix could achieve. On 125 requests one request is
    72	worth 0.8\,pp, so the margin is about one request. The tier's
    73	segment-to-segment SD (5.1 to 21.4\,pp) is not the right yardstick: segment
    74	volatility is not run-to-run uncertainty. The right one is the replicate spread
    75	we have---which, across the five configurations we repeated, is 0.5, 0.8, 1.0,
    76	3.9 and 5.1\,pp (\texttt{replicates.csv}). Against the two tightest of those $+0.9$\,pp is about a replicate
    77	wide; against the three widest---which belong to the configurations that decide
    78	our headline elsewhere---it is inside one. We quote the tightest pair
    79	because it is the least favourable to us, not because it is the whole
    80	distribution. What we will defend is the weaker claim: on the
    81	low-load tier the controller is not worse, and the same switch that is worth
    82	$+51\%$ there costs $-42\%$ at high load. A controller that is competitive
    83	where load is light and dominated where load is heavy is the pattern this
    84	paper set out to explain, and it is the pattern we measured.
    85	
    86	The second finding is the more useful one. The signal an adaptive controller
    87	would need is available---the number of sessions waiting for admission is four
    88	times larger on the 200-session tier than on the same-generation 25-session arm
    89	(median 66 against 16, maximum 135 against 25; the earlier-harness 25-session
    90	arms of \S\ref{sec:load} read 9--11, which is where the 6--7$\times$ of an
    91	earlier draft came from, and we quote the same-generation pair)---but its
    92	\emph{interpretation} is not obvious. At the 25-session tier the controller
    93	holds sixteen sessions back while running one; that backlog is manufactured by
    94	the throttling itself rather than
    95	evidence that demand has slackened. Reading it correctly requires combining
    96	instantaneous backlog with progress through the run, which is a stateful
    97	computation a cap cannot perform. We regard this as the most promising
    98	direction we did not finish, and we are explicit that we did not finish it.
    99	
   100	\begin{figure}[tb]
   101	  \centering
   102	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig5_loadtier.pdf}
   103	  \caption{The same throttle switch, two load tiers, opposite signs. The
   104	  25-session bars are from an earlier harness revision and are shown as a
   105	  direction, not a magnitude---the current-harness 25-session comparison is in
   106	  Table~\ref{tab:tier25} instead. The 200-session bars are current. The two
   107	  panels have independent axes, so the bars are not comparable in height
   108	  across panels---the annotations give each panel's own change.}
   109	  \label{fig:loadtier}
   110	\end{figure}
   111	
   112	\section{Related Work}
   113	\label{sec:related}
   114	
   115	\paragraph{Admission and scheduling for agentic serving.} Recent systems
   116	report large gains from treating an agent session, rather than a request, as
   117	the schedulable unit, and from preserving session state across tool calls:
   118	session-aware admission with CPU-restorable KV
   119	\cite{talaria,continuum}, program-level pause/resume with shortest-context-first
   120	restoration \cite{thunderagent}, and congestion-based control over an
   121	agent-level window \cite{concur}. A recurring measurement in this line is that
   122	recomputation, not admission, dominates: \cite{concur} attributes 49.1\% of
   123	end-to-end latency to recomputation, and \cite{mori} preserves state on the
   124	CPU at the cost of a reload. Our results are consistent with this literature
   125	rather than in tension with it, and \S\ref{sec:discussion} explains why: the
   126	mechanism these systems add is one our controller already has.
   127	
   128	\paragraph{Engine-side techniques.} A separate line improves the engine
   129	rather than the admission decision. Iteration-level batching and paged KV
   130	\cite{yu2022orca,kwon2023pagedattention} are the substrate every policy here runs
   131	on; chunked prefill with stall-free batching \cite{sarathi}, prefill/decode
   132	disaggregation \cite{distserve}, and cache-centric scheduling
   133	\cite{mooncake} are the refinements. Our \S\ref{sec:model} measures 2.6--8.2\,ms of each
   134	27--37\,ms step as work that is not decoding---prefill and non-decode
   135	overhead---which is exactly what this line attacks. We expect these techniques to move the frontier rather than to decide who
   136	sits above it, but we did not run the interaction and so state it as an
   137	expectation.
   138	
   139	\paragraph{State retention and preemption.} A third line keeps session state
   140	alive across the gaps we measure in \S\ref{sec:model}: keeping KV on cheaper
   141	storage across turns \cite{yu2025pensieve,gao2024cachedattention}, migrating it
   142	between instances \cite{sun2024llumnix}, and preempting at iteration granularity
   143	\cite{wu2026fastserve}. These systems exercise finer control than our 2\,s
   144	admission cycle, which is the boundary of our negative result: we falsify one
   145	control granularity, not the family. What they share with \sys{} is that the
   146	state is not discarded---which is why, as we argue below, adding admission
   147	intelligence on top buys nothing here.
   148	
   149	\paragraph{Application-level dependencies and replay.} Serving an LLM
   150	\emph{application} rather than a request means the scheduler sees dependencies
   151	between calls \cite{lin2024parrot}, and agentic traces have structure that
   152	request-level benchmarks do not capture \cite{wang2026xperf}. Our replay
   153	preserves that structure but fixes it in advance, which is exactly the
   154	limitation these works would press on.
   155	
   156	\paragraph{Prefix caching.} The prefix cache is often proposed as the signal
   157	an admission policy should use. What we measure is that the \emph{realized}
   158	reuse is a function of the admission policy and of the host tier, while the
   159	\emph{planned} reuse is a constant of the trace; an earlier draft of this
   160	paper conflated the two and got the direction wrong in both. The trace plans
   161	a round-1-and-later hit rate of 80.9\%. Without HiCache the realized
   162	round-1+ rate follows the plan only at the loosest cap and then collapses:
   163	80.8\%, 78.5\%, 70.6\%, 56.0\%, 32.8\% for caps of one through five. With
   164	HiCache it tracks the plan at every cap we ran: 80.7\%, 79.6\%, 78.7\% for
   165	caps of two, three and four. Our controller sits at 80.6\%.
   166	So the honest statement is not that admission does not move this quantity---%
   167	\texttt{cap4} alone moves it 25 points---but that the host tier is what keeps
   168	it from moving, which is the same mechanism \S\ref{sec:model} and
   169	\S\ref{sec:criterion} keep running into. A configuration without a host tier
   170	that admits four sessions does not have the cache that a host-tier one at the
   171	same concurrency does. All eight numbers in this paragraph are the
   172	\emph{observed} per-step hit rate (\texttt{server\_prefix\_hit\_rate} in the
   173	per-step logs), unweighted mean over rounds $\ge 1$; the planned rate is the
   174	trace constant 80.9\% and does not move with the cap. \S\ref{sec:model} uses
   175	a different convention---all steps, token-weighted, so that reuse and
   176	recomputation partition the prompt---under which the same collapse reads
   177	92.5\%$\to$36.4\% of the trace's bound for the device-only caps and
   178	92.4\%$\to$90.2\% for the static host-tier caps. We state both because the paper's earlier
   179	drafts mixed them. We note the boundary of this claim---we varied admission policy,
   180	not eviction policy, and SGLang exposes alternate eviction policies we did not
   181	test. A characterization of production KV-cache traffic at a large cloud
   182	provider finds that eviction-policy design is ``highly workload-dependent''
   183	and that reuse patterns differ sharply by request category
   184	\cite{wang2025kvcachewild}; that is reason to expect a single trace family to
   185	understate the space, though it is not a policy comparison and we do not
   186	present it as one.
   187	
   188	\section{Discussion}
   189	\label{sec:discussion}
   190	
   191	\paragraph{Why published gains do not reappear.} The systems cited above win
   192	by keeping session state alive across tool waits and by pausing at agent
   193	granularity rather than request granularity. \sys{} has both properties by
   194	construction: the permit file gives per-session pause and resume, and the
   195	controller never frees capacity by discarding a session---it admits fewer
   196	sessions instead. To that extent the mechanism these systems add is one our
   197	controller already has, which is one reason adding admission intelligence on
   198	top of it buys nothing here.
   199	
   200	We are careful not to overstate this, because the distinction is between
   201	\emph{choosing} to discard state and \emph{having} it evicted. Retention here
   202	is the engine's radix cache doing its ordinary job; it is not something the
   203	controller implements, and \S\ref{sec:model} measures that the cache evicts
   204	parked prefixes under pressure and that they are recomputed. What \sys{}
   205	avoids is the deliberate trade of state for capacity. A system that manages the
   206	host tier as an explicit policy---as \cite{mori} and \cite{yu2025pensieve}
   207	do---holds a lever we do not, and our negative result does not cover it.
   208	
   209	\paragraph{The threshold decides the verdict, and we report that.} Every SLO
   210	number in this paper counts a request as good if its time-to-first-token is
   211	under 10\,s. That constant sets the whole vertical axis, so we re-derived the
   212	frontier at 5\,s and 20\,s as well, from the same per-request logs. The
   213	ordering of the static caps does not change. What changes is where the
   214	controllers sit relative to them, and at the loose end one of them crosses:
   215	
   216	\begin{center}
   217	\small
   218	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
   219	\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
   220	  \toprule
   221	  SLO threshold & 5\,s & 10\,s & 20\,s \\
   222	  \midrule
   223	  \texttt{horizon}=1        & $-2.9$ & $-0.9$ & $+1.5$ \\
   224	  \sys{} base               & $-2.3$ & $-1.3$ & $+0.6$ \\
   225	  \texttt{bres}             & $-3.6$ & $-1.4$ & $+0.3$ \\
   226	  Concur-style AIMD        & $-14.2$ & $-11.2$ & $-6.5$ \\
   227	  \bottomrule
   228	\end{tabular}
   229	\end{center}
   230	
   231	Two readings, and we give both. The first is that our headline claim is
   232	conditional: at the strict threshold that interactive serving actually cares
   233	about, no controller reaches the static front; at a loose one, one does. The
   234	second is that the crossing is still not a win, though the reason is more
   235	awkward than we first wrote. $+1.5$\,pp at 20\,s is larger than the replicate
   236	SLO spread of the three tightest pairs we have---0.5\,pp for the base
   237	controller, 0.8\,pp for \texttt{cap3}+HiCache and 1.0\,pp for
   238	\texttt{cap2}+HiCache---but smaller than the two widest, 3.9\,pp for the best
   239	arm's own repeat and 5.1\,pp for \texttt{cap4}+HiCache, and those two are
   240	anchors. It is the anchors, not the controllers, that set the line the margin
   241	is measured against, so the comparison that matters is the one against the
   242	widest anchor---where the margin is not resolvable. (An earlier revision discarded
   243	the \texttt{cap3}+HiCache pair on the ground that one run had exceeded its
   244	cap. That was wrong: the over-cap run is the \emph{device-only}
   245	\texttt{cap3} repeat, and both HiCache runs peak at three with none over. The
   246	pair is a valid replicate and we use it, including as an envelope anchor.)
   247	A margin that appears only at one threshold
   248	and only at a size we cannot resolve is not evidence that adaptive admission
   249	works; it is evidence that the question needs more runs than we have. We state
   250	the conditional version in the abstract, and we flag this as the second thing
   251	we would re-run with more GPU time.
   252	
   253	\paragraph{The SLO metric excludes the controller's own cost, and we checked.}
   254	Every SLO number above counts a request as good if its time-to-first-token is
   255	under 10\,s, and the runner measures that from the moment the request is
   256	\emph{submitted to the engine}. A session that waits for a permit has not been
   257	submitted yet, so the wait is invisible---and that wait is precisely the cost
   258	an admission controller imposes. We therefore recomputed the runs we have per-step
   259	logs for on a ready-to-first-token basis, where a round's clock starts when the
   260	session is actually able to run (its arrival for round~0, the previous round's
   261	completion plus its tool wait otherwise). That is nineteen of the twenty-five
   262	200-session runs in Table~\ref{tab:runs}; absent are the no-control baseline,
   263	the two HiCache anchor repeats and three controller repeats, and we do not
   264	extend the claim to them.
   265	
   266	The correction is large. Every configuration loses 2.9--19.3\,pp of SLO, and the
   267	loss is systematic: tight caps queue the hardest, so \texttt{cap1} falls from
   268	94.8\% to 76.2\% while \texttt{cap5} falls only from 27.1\% to 24.2\%. A
   269	session admitted last under \texttt{cap1} can wait hours for its turn; its
   270	engine-side TTFT is still 1.6\,s.
   271	
   272	What does \emph{not} change is the verdict, within the subset we recomputed.
   273	The static envelope keeps the same four anchors---the two anchor
   274	repeats, which we did not recompute on the ready clock, stand in as their first
   275	runs, and every distance below carries that substitution
   276	(\texttt{arrival\_slo.csv} records the columns)---and of the eleven controller
   277	arms, nine move by no more than
   278	2.5\,pp. Two move more---the all-throttles-off arm improves by 8.2\,pp and the
   279	retuned AIMD arm by 3.2\,pp---and both stay below the front either way. One
   280	crosses the $\pm1.0$\,pp band, and it is the one that decides our headline:
   281	\texttt{horizon}=1 goes from $-0.93$ (inside the band, a tie) to $-1.21$ (below
   282	it), because on the ready clock its own SLO falls from 84.8\% to 68.2\% while
   283	the anchor it is measured against falls less. The ready clock therefore moves
   284	it from a tie to a loss, which is the same direction the paragraph above
   285	reaches from the other side, and it is why we do not report a ready-clock
   286	distance for it as a headline: the two anchor repeats that define the line are
   287	not in the set we could recompute (\S\ref{sec:discussion} says
   288	which runs are), and inventing a line for them would be the same error this
   289	subsection is about.
   290	
   291	The subset matters and we state its edge. The two configurations whose repeats
   292	decide how we read the verdict---\texttt{horizon}=1's repeat and the fastest
   293	anchor \texttt{cap4}+HC's---are not in the nineteen, because we did not
   294	recompute their per-step logs on the ready clock; the rebuilt envelope
   295	therefore rests on the original anchors without their repeats, and a
   296	ready-clock frontier that included them could move the line by the 3.9 and
   297	5.1\,pp those repeats differ by. We do not claim the verdict is unchanged under
   298	every SLO definition---only that on the runs we could recompute, moving the
   299	clock to the ready point costs every configuration 3--19\,pp, moves no
   300	configuration from below the line to above it, and changes exactly one
   301	controller verdict: \texttt{horizon}=1 leaves the tie band on the below
   302	side. (One static cap crosses the other way, \texttt{cap3}+HC from $-1.72$ to
   303	$-0.79$, which does not help any controller.)
   304	We report this because a reviewer should ask it,
   305	and because the answer is the useful part: the negative result is not an
   306	artifact of choosing an SLO definition that flatters controllers. If anything
   307	the flattering choice was the one that moved the arm nearest the line from
   308	$-0.9$ to $+1.5$---that is, from just below the line to just above the tie
   309	band's upper edge, by a margin of the same order as that pair's own replicate
   310	spread (3.9\,pp).
   311	
   312	\paragraph{Limitations.} Our results come from one 24\,GB accelerator and one
   313	7B model. The workload axis we did test once more (\S\ref{sec:xtrace}); the
   314	hardware axis we did not, so generality across devices is untested. Only five configurations
   315	have been repeated---four of them twice and the base configuration four
   316	times---so the replicate spread we report in \S\ref{sec:results} is estimated
   317	from one to three degrees of freedom per configuration; a margin as small as the one that decides our verdict would need several
   318	more repeats per configuration to resolve, and we did not run them. Two specific boundaries
   319	matter. First, the pool holds about four median sessions, so concurrency is
   320	capacity-bound by construction; on a device with a much larger pool the
   321	premise may not hold and the conclusion may invert---we regard reproducing on
   322	a larger device as the single most important next experiment. Second, the
   323	load is stationary, single-tenant and single-SLO. The load-dependence result
   324	of \S\ref{sec:load} is measured on two \emph{separate} constant-load tiers, so
   325	it says what to expect when conditions differ, not what happens while they
   326	change. We did run the experiment that would settle that---a trace whose load
   327	moves mid-run, with a static cap on each tier and a switcher that changes its
   328	cap at the trace's designed switch point---and we cannot report a result from
   329	it, in either direction, because its static baselines were measured under
   330	host co-tenancy: \texttt{var\_cap3} spent 158 of its 266 minutes at four or
   331	more concurrent engine instances, and \texttt{var\_cap2} is flagged as a
   332	watch (\S\ref{sec:repro}). A first look at those runs has the switcher
   333	finishing 15 minutes ahead of the best static cap at an SLO inside the tie
   334	band, which would be the first evidence in this paper that adaptivity pays;
   335	we do not report it as a result, because the baseline it beats is precisely
   336	the one the audit flags, and a 60\%-contaminated baseline is inflated in
   337	exactly the direction that manufactures that finding. Clean re-runs of that
   338	family are in flight, and the honest position until they land is that the
   339	variable-load case is measured and unusable, not unmeasured. Multi-tenant and
   340	heterogeneous-SLO settings are where we would look next.
   341	
   342	\paragraph{Reproducibility.}
   343	\label{sec:repro}
   344	The front and the signed distance of each controller are computed by
   345	\texttt{scripts/lab/frontier.py} from the raw per-step logs; Table~\ref{tab:runs}
   346	is rendered by \texttt{scripts/lab/gen\_table.py} from
   347	\texttt{frontier.py}'s full-precision coordinates
   348	(\texttt{paper/data/runs\_precise.csv}), including the four runs that stopped
   349	short of 999 steps; the live-session column comes from the runner's admission
   350	log via \texttt{run\_summary.py}, and \S\ref{sec:criterion} is computed by
   351	\texttt{gen\_elasticity.py} and \texttt{gen\_elasticity\_hc.py}. Every run is
   352	guarded against a shrunken KV pool.
   353	
   354	\emph{Co-tenancy on the measurement host.} Our runs share a host with other
   355	tenants and with each other, and late in the campaign we found that this
   356	matters more than the pool guard catches. An audit
   357	(\texttt{gen\_concurrency.py}, data in \texttt{paper/data/concurrency.csv})
   358	reconstructs, for every run, how many other runs were alive for how long. Runs
   359	that overlapped three or more others for at least an hour are flagged;
   360	fourteen of ours are, two of which are failed runs we do not report. The flag is a risk marker, not a measurement of damage,
   361	and we treat it that way: five of the twelve (\texttt{F:t85}, \texttt{F:t90},
   362	\texttt{var\_foyer}, \texttt{cap1} and the published-parameter AIMD row) show
   363	no aggregate inflation at all, because their overlap was short relative to the
   364	run or did not touch their decode interval. Where the damage is legible it is large---the
   365	per-request decode interval $\tau_2$, measured client-side and never touching
   366	the sampler, sits at 30.9--33.3\,ms for the three affected arms still carrying
   367	the flag in
   368	Table~\ref{tab:runs} against 23.8--25.7\,ms for the same family measured
   369	clean. A fourth affected arm has since been re-measured alone and came in at
   370	24.8\,ms, inside the clean range, which is the correction in action. Two
   371	controls pin the cause to co-tenancy rather than to the configuration. One
   372	run (\texttt{cap1}) holds $\tau_2$ at 20.3--20.7\,ms for its first five and a
   373	half hours and jumps to 22.4--27.5\,ms in the minute two other runs start. And
   374	two independent runs (\texttt{F:t85}, \texttt{F:rl99}) step down from
   375	$\approx$30\,ms to 22--25\,ms in the same wall-clock minute, when a third run
   376	ends. Fourteen of our runs fall in the flagged class; five of them are rows of
   377	Table~\ref{tab:runs} (\texttt{cap1}, \texttt{F:hz0},
   378	\texttt{F:hz2}, \texttt{F:breshz1}, and the published-parameter AIMD row) and
   379	nine are arms we do not report here. The flag is a window, and only three of
   380	those five are actually damaged---the controller variants whose $\tau_2$
   381	is 30.9--33.3\,ms. \texttt{cap1} and the AIMD row show no aggregate
   382	inflation (20.6 and 24.4\,ms), so we keep their numbers and flag the window.
   383	A sixth row carried the flag and the damage, and has been re-measured alone
   384	under the same one-run-per-GPU discipline: \texttt{bres}, whose clean
   385	$\tau_2$ is 24.8\,ms. Its row in Table~\ref{tab:runs} now carries the clean
   386	numbers, and the two conclusions that rested on it---its step-cost
   387	attribution in \S\ref{sec:results:attribution} and the route-1 comparison of
   388	\S\ref{sec:results}---are restated in that section rather than left
   389	provisional. The remaining three damaged rows are still being re-measured,
   390	and until those runs land we treat their wall-clock, SLO and step-cost
   391	columns as unusable: the signed distances in Table~\ref{tab:runs} for those
   392	three rows and the $\tau$-high attributions of
   393	\S\ref{sec:results:attribution} where it names them. We flag them rather than
   394	quietly dropping the rows so that a reader can see which conclusions are still
   395	standing on which measurements.
   396	
   397	Two things we can already say. First, the failure is silent: the engine-side
   398	counters---running count, pool occupancy, evictions, recomputation---are
   399	indistinguishable between the affected runs and the clean ones, so nothing in
   400	the ordinary telemetry would have caught this. Second, the affected runs'
   401	\emph{volume} columns look untouched: the three damaged host-tier arms reuse
   402	0.6791--0.6959 of the prompt against 0.5497--0.6981 across the sixteen
   403	undamaged host-tier arms, so the damaged runs sit inside the clean range
   404	rather than apart from it---suggestive, but not proof, since co-tenancy could
   405	in principle also reorder completions and change eviction order.
   406	
   407	The one arm we have both ways settles the second point directly. \texttt{bres}
   408	measured under co-tenancy reuses 0.6943 of its prompt; the same policy measured
   409	alone reuses 0.6904, a 0.39-point difference, while its per-request decode
   410	interval moves 31.3 to 24.8\,ms and its wall clock by 10\%. The volume columns
   411	therefore carry no signature of the contamination at all, which is exactly why
   412	the audit had to run on the client-side clock. For the three arms still
   413	outstanding, the re-runs will confirm it policy by policy, and we compare
   414	reuse per round and per session between the pairs rather than only the totals.
   415	
   416	One caveat about the controller artifact, which a reader with our code would
   417	otherwise hit. There is no single controller copy behind this paper: the runs
   418	record four different hashes of \texttt{controller\_budget3.py}
   419	(\texttt{2b573829083c} on 31 runs, \texttt{f825bc768188} on 24,
   420	\texttt{d692ab11f396} on 4 and \texttt{e1dd5ae78019} on 3), so the artifact
   421	evolved during the campaign and a reader cannot reproduce every number from
   422	one revision. None of the four matches the repository copy
   423	(\texttt{a21bd2b63774}), and the difference is not one hunk but several: the
   424	queue-scan fix described in \S\ref{sec:results:gate}, and a rename of the
   425	logging variable whose rebinding \S\ref{sec:results} identifies as the source
   426	of a retraction. The repository has both; none of the four running copies has
   427	either.
   428	The numbers in this paper therefore describe the pre-fix path, with one
   429	partial exception we want to state precisely rather than let a reader assume
   430	the stronger thing. We did run the same configuration with \texttt{sf=0}
   431	(\S\ref{sec:results:idle-memory}), and on the running copy that changes two
   432	things at once: it scans the queue \emph{and} drops the one-per-cycle limit,
   433	because the \texttt{break} that enforces single-flight is inside the same
   434	conditional. The repository's fix keeps the limit and only adds the scan. So our
   435	run is not a test of the fix as written---it is a test of the fix plus a looser
   436	admission rate, and it moves the arm along the front rather than past it. The
   437	narrower experiment, scan-with-the-limit-kept, is the one we would do first with
   438	more GPU time. We report six claims we retracted during
   439	the work, because the retractions
   440	were the most informative events. In order of how much they cost us:
   441	
   442	\emph{The denominator of the step cost.} Our first \S\ref{sec:model} divided by
   443	the whole-run mean running count while multiplying by busy time. The two are
   444	not the same quantity---the whole-run mean includes the idle samples---so
   445	$\tau$ came out low by exactly the busy fraction, 36\% for \texttt{cap1}, and
   446	two independent errors cancelled to make our two measurement methods appear to
   447	agree. This is the one that changed a headline number.
   448	
   449	\emph{A mechanism refutation that was itself refuted}: we withdrew the
   450	eviction-to-recomputation account on the strength of two checks that were both
   451	identities rather than measurements (\S\ref{sec:model}).
   452	
   453	\emph{A median-based reading of the tool wait} that inverted a central
   454	assumption. \emph{A monotone relation between idle fraction and concurrency}
   455	that the full data does not show. \emph{A growth-sensitivity conclusion} that
   456	rested on an arm we had already invalidated. And \emph{the accounting
   457	double-charge} of \S\ref{sec:results}, falsified twice---once by source
   458	inspection and once by the end-to-end measurement here.
   459	
   460	\section{Conclusion}
   461	
   462	We asked whether an adaptive admission controller can beat a tuned static
   463	concurrency cap on a memory-bound agentic LLM workload, and found that on our
   464	system it cannot: nine variants, four routes tested, none above the static
   465	front. We expected the reason to be a cancellation---admission policy controls
   466	how many requests are in flight, the per-step cost rises with that number, and
   467	the product is what the wall clock sees---and we built a two-part decomposition
   468	of the wall clock that makes the cancellation measurable. That decomposition
   469	holds, and the cost it measures is real, but it is not the whole reason and on
   470	the configuration our controllers run it is not the binding one: there the
   471	elasticity stays below one across the operating range, so added concurrency was
   472	still cheap and the engine had room. Our controllers did not take it. What we
   473	can say at the end is narrower than what we set out to say: on this hardware and
   474	this workload, the admission rules we could build and tune did not beat a static
   475	cap, the capacity estimate they act on is 1.79$\times$ the truth, and a
   476	structural idle component that no policy we built recovers at a profit remains.
   477	The regime in which adaptivity should have paid---where the same switch is worth
   478	$+51\%$ and $-42\%$---is one where the controller is not dominated by any static
   479	point we measured, and does not dominate one either. The measurement we offer is
   480	a diagnostic an engineer can take before committing to a sweep; whether it
   481	predicts the outcome on another workload is a question we tested once and could
   482	not confirm.
   483	
   484	The part we would carry elsewhere is not the negative result but the
   485	mechanism behind it. Prefix reuse is bounded by the workload---75.5\% of our
   486	prompt tokens---and whether that bound survives added concurrency is set by
   487	the memory tier: device-only, realization falls from 92.5\% to 36.4\% as the
   488	running count rises, and the shortfall reappears as recomputation; with a host
   489	tier the static caps hold at 90.2--92.4\% across the range our controllers
   490	occupy, and the worst of the nineteen host-tier runs that completed this
   491	trace---the one that disables every throttle---still realizes 72.8\%, where the
   492	nearest device-only point, the cap
   493	of four at $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.65, manages 60.7\%---while
   494	the device cache misses \emph{more}. Two configurations at the same running
   495	count and the same pool occupancy realize 79.0\% and 91.3\% of the bound,
   496	which is why we describe the tier rather than the occupancy as the variable.
   497	That claim is measured on one trace family and one card, its controlled
   498	version is still running, and we do not extend it past what we measured---but
   499	it is the finding a system designer can act on, and it is the one we would
   500	test first on different hardware. Two boundaries come with it. On a second
   501	workload both of our measurements survive in direction and neither in level:
   502	that trace realizes less of its own bound at a lower occupancy, and its
   503	elasticity turns positive at a lower running count---reaching 1.0 there where
   504	the main trace reaches 1.15 at 1.53---so neither an occupancy threshold nor a
   505	concurrency threshold transfers. And the variable-load case, which is where a controller should
   506	have its best chance, is measured but not usable---its baselines were taken
   507	under host co-tenancy and we report it as open rather than as a result.

     1	\section{Introduction}
     2	\label{sec:intro}
     3	
     4	An agentic LLM session is not a request. It is a long-lived job that issues a
     5	round of inference, waits on a tool, and comes back---tens of times---with a
     6	context that grows every round. On a memory-constrained accelerator this makes
     7	admission control look like the obvious lever. The KV pool is the binding
     8	resource; a single large session can occupy most of it; and the sessions
     9	themselves are wildly heterogeneous. If a scheduler can see how much of the
    10	pool a candidate will need and how much room is left, surely it should beat a
    11	fixed number.
    12	
    13	We believed this, built a controller that does exactly that, and spent a
    14	considerable amount of GPU time discovering that on our workload and hardware
    15	it is false. This paper reports that negative result, and the negative result is
    16	most of what we have. Alongside it we report one measurement---the elasticity of
    17	per-step cost with respect to the running count---that we hoped would say in
    18	advance how much room admission control has. It does bound that room on the
    19	configurations we measured, but when we tested whether it explains our
    20	controllers' failure, on their own configuration and on a second workload, it
    21	did not. We report it as a diagnostic of a workload, not as a predictor of
    22	whether a controller will win.
    23	
    24	\subsection{What we measured}
    25	
    26	We serve a replay of 200 real coding-agent sessions on a single RTX 3090
    27	(Qwen2.5-Coder-7B, 96K context, SGLang, a 101{,}432-token KV pool) and compare
    28	a family of admission policies against a sweep of static concurrency caps.
    29	Nine controller variants, spanning four mechanistically distinct ideas, all
    30	land below the static Pareto front, and so does a reimplementation of an
    31	independent congestion-based controller from the literature
    32	(Fig.~\ref{fig:frontier}). The best of them misses by under a point---inside the
    33	$\pm1.0$\,pp band we call a tie, but on the below side of it---and the closest
    34	arm we repeated misses by 0.9 with its repeat at 5.6.
    35	
    36	\begin{figure}[tb]
    37	  \centering
    38	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig1_frontier.pdf}
    39	  \caption{Every completed run on the (wall clock, SLO) plane. The grey line
    40	  is the upper-left envelope of the static points---drawn from the data, so it
    41	  uses each configuration's \emph{better} run where a repeat exists, which is
    42	  why \texttt{cap3}+HiCache's 236-minute run sits below the line rather than on
    43	  it; a policy below or on it has
    44	  not beaten a static cap, because time-slicing two caps approximates any
    45	  point on the segment between them. Nine \sys{} variants plus a
    46	  reimplementation of an independent controller, none above at the 10\,s
    47	  latency threshold used throughout; \S\ref{sec:discussion} reports how far
    48	  that verdict is conditional on the threshold. The five points drawn with a
    49	  hollow ring were measured while three or more other engine instances shared
    50	  the host (\S\ref{sec:repro}); on three of them the per-request decode
    51	  interval is inflated by 25--40\% and they are not comparable with the rest,
    52	  on the other two the flag is a window that left no measurable trace. The
    53	  three are being re-measured, and one arm that carried the flag in an
    54	  earlier version has been re-measured clean and is plotted without a ring.}
    55	  \label{fig:frontier}
    56	\end{figure}
    57	
    58	\subsection{Why}
    59	
    60	We decompose the wall clock into \emph{busy} time, during which the engine has
    61	at least one request in flight, and \emph{idle} time, during which it has
    62	none. The busy component is governed by $W_{\text{busy}} \approx
    63	(G/\overline{R})\,\tau$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:busy}), where $\overline{R}$ is the mean
    64	number of running requests, $G$ the generated tokens and $\tau$ the per-step
    65	cost. An admission policy moves $\overline{R}$, and on our configurations it
    66	moves little else---though \S\ref{sec:model} gives two arms where the busy and
    67	idle components move in \emph{opposite} directions, which is why the split is
    68	worth making at all.
    69	The difficulty is that $\tau$ is not constant in $\overline{R}$: across our
    70	runs $\tau_1$ spans 27.0--36.7\,ms, and on the device-only family, raising the
    71	busy-time running count from 1.00 to 1.84 absorbs a third of what the extra
    72	concurrency buys.
    73	
    74	The reason $\tau$ moves is recomputation, and whether recomputation happens is
    75	decided by the memory tier rather than by the policy. A session's prefix is
    76	worth reusing only while it is still resident, so prefix \emph{reuse} is
    77	bounded by the workload---in our trace, 75.5\% of the prompt tokens, a bound
    78	no configuration we ran reaches. How much of that bound is realized is where
    79	the tier enters. With the KV pool on the device alone, realization falls as
    80	concurrency rises---92.5\% of the bound at $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.00
    81	down to 36.4\% at 1.84---and the shortfall appears as recomputation, 8.6\,M to
    82	20.7\,M tokens. With a host tier in front of it the decline disappears: over the
    83	running counts our controllers occupy, the static points fall only from 92.4\%
    84	to 90.2\%, and the worst of the nineteen host-tier runs---the one that disables
    85	every throttle---still realizes 72.8\%, where the nearest device-only point
    86	(the cap of four, at $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.65) manages 60.7\%, while
    87	the \emph{device} misses more. Two configurations at the same running
    88	count and the same pool occupancy realize 79.0\% and 91.3\% of the bound
    89	respectively (\S\ref{sec:model}); what separates them is not how full the pool
    90	is but what happens when it overflows. That pair also differs in date and in
    91	GPU, so a capacity control that changes the pool size alone on one card is
    92	queued behind the re-runs; \S\ref{sec:results} reports it when it lands.
    93	That is the finding we would carry to another system first, and it is why this
    94	paper's answer to its own title is a property of the deployment rather than of
    95	the controller.
    96	
    97	The idle component is structural rather than a control failure. A session in a
    98	tool wait issues no request, so when all live sessions happen to be waiting,
    99	the engine is genuinely empty. The stretches are short in the middle and long
   100	in the tail---mean 20.9\,s---and the tail is what a scheduler feels: 69.9\%
   101	of all idle \emph{time} sits in stretches over 20\,s, an order of magnitude
   102	above the 2\,s control cycle. (The median is not a good summary here: it moves
   103	between 5 and 10\,s depending on which runs are pooled, because one long run
   104	contributes a fifth of the stretches.) Crucially, the tool wait itself is much longer than
   105	the trace's median suggests: the median inter-round gap is 0.35\,s, but the
   106	\emph{mean} is 15.8--16.9\,s with 22\% of gaps over ten seconds. A controller
   107	that
   108	reads only the median concludes, as we initially did, that sessions never
   109	leave and therefore that there is no idle time to recover. There is.
   110	
   111	\subsection{Contributions}
   112	
   113	\begin{itemize}
   114	  \item \textbf{We identify the memory tier as what decides how much of the
   115	        achievable prefix reuse survives added concurrency, and therefore how
   116	        much a controller has to gain by admitting more.} The two senses of
   117	        ``room'' need separating, and \S\ref{sec:xtrace} is what separates
   118	        them: the tier bounds the \emph{local} return on added concurrency
   119	        (busy-time throughput), and it does not by itself decide whether a
   120	        controller wins \emph{end to end}, where idle time and the SLO axis
   121	        also move. Our second workload is the demonstration---it has no host
   122	        tier and its controller runs at low concurrency (1.22), the two
   123	        conditions this section associates with room, and it still does not win
   124	        (\S\ref{sec:xtrace}). The claim here is the local one. The trace
   125	        bounds reuse at
   126	        75.5\% of its prompt tokens, and no configuration we ran reaches that
   127	        bound---the best realizes 92.5\% of it. What the tier decides is how
   128	        much of the bound is realized as concurrency rises: device-only,
   129	        realization falls from 92.5\% to 36.4\% between
   130	        $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.00 and 1.84 and the shortfall appears
   131	        as recomputation, 8.6\,M to 20.7\,M tokens; with a host tier it holds
   132	        at 90.2--92.4\% over 1.25 to 1.73, while the \emph{device} misses more
   133	        (\S\ref{sec:model}). Two configurations at the same running count and
   134	        the same pool occupancy realize 79.0\% and 91.3\% of the bound, so it
   135	        is not how full the pool is but what happens when it overflows; that
   136	        pair differs in date and GPU as well, so a capacity control that
   137	        varies the pool alone on one card is queued; \S\ref{sec:results}
   138	        reports it when it lands. The per-step elasticity follows the same line:
   139	        it passes one above $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}} \approx 1.5$
   140	        device-only and stays below one across the controllers' whole
   141	        operating range with a host tier (\S\ref{sec:criterion}). Two
   142	        measurements that do not share a code path---client-side step timing
   143	        and engine-side token accounting---give the same answer. This is the
   144	        finding we would carry to another system first, and it is what the
   145	        title's question turns on.
   146	  \item We test four routes by which an adaptive admission controller was
   147	        expected to beat a tuned static cap on this workload, and we are
   148	        precise about what each one rests on. Two are falsified by measurement
   149	        against criteria we fixed before the runs---changing the accounting
   150	        base (\S\ref{sec:results}) and changing the growth reserve. One is a
   151	        knob rather than a hypothesis, and we report it only because it slides
   152	        the operating point along the front and adds no mechanism. The fourth,
   153	        changing the execution granularity from session count to
   154	        running-request count, is falsified by measurement \emph{and} by an
   155	        argument about why the permit mechanism cannot express the control it
   156	        would need. We do not call the last two of these falsifications of a
   157	        stated hypothesis, because they were not.
   158	  \item We give a two-part decomposition of the wall clock (\S\ref{sec:model})
   159	        that locates the cancellation: admission policy moves the
   160	        running-request count, and the per-step cost moves with it. On the
   161	        device-only configurations we can account for that rise token for
   162	        token---evicted prefixes are recomputed almost one for one. On
   163	        host-tier configurations recomputation is flat and the account has to
   164	        come from elsewhere; we report the part of that residual we were able
   165	        to attribute, and the part we were not (\S\ref{sec:model},
   166	        \S\ref{sec:repro}).
   167	  \item We reduce the question to one measurable number, the elasticity of
   168	        per-step cost with respect to the running count (\S\ref{sec:criterion}),
   169	        and measure it at $0.38$ on the model-consistent step cost and $0.61$
   170	        on the per-request one, on the configurations whose mechanism we can
   171	        account for token for token. We offer it as a diagnostic rather than a
   172	        predictor: it says where on the curve a policy operates and how much
   173	        the remaining headroom is worth, and \S\ref{sec:criterion} is explicit
   174	        about the sense in which it is circular and the sense in which it is
   175	        not.
   176	  \item We characterize when adaptivity \emph{could} have paid, and show that
   177	        our workload is not that case: the same throttle configuration that is
   178	        worth $+51\%$ at 25 sessions costs $-42\%$ at 200 (\S\ref{sec:load}).
   179	        Put differently, no single scalar cap is optimal across both, which is
   180	        the regime adaptive control exists for. On the low-load tier, where
   181	        that argument bites hardest, the controller is \emph{not} worse---%
   182	        same-generation measurements put it on the front, $+0.9$\,pp above the
   183	        line and inside the noise. On the high-load tier it is dominated. That
   184	        asymmetry, not a blanket failure, is what this paper measures.
   185	  \item We make the measurements reproducible: the front and each controller's
   186	        signed distance are produced by one script from the raw per-step logs,
   187	        the tables and the sub-thousand-step runs by two others, all named in
   188	        \S\ref{sec:repro}. We report the six claims we had to retract along the
   189	        way, with the checks that caught them.
   190	\end{itemize}
   191	
   192	\subsection{What we do not claim}
   193	
   194	We do not claim that admission control is useless in general. We claim it does
   195	not pay \emph{here} and we show the frontier measurement that decides that; the
   196	diagnostic of \S\ref{sec:criterion} is a cheaper check an engineer can run
   197	before committing to a sweep, not a verdict on its own, and it can be taken on another
   198	workload before committing to a sweep. We also do not claim our hardware and model are
   199	representative; our results are from one 24\,GB accelerator and one 7B model,
   200	and extending them is future work.

     1	\section{Background and Setup}
     2	\label{sec:bg}
     3	
     4	\subsection{Workload}
     5	
     6	We replay 200 coding-agent sessions drawn from the {\sc TraceLab} corpus
     7	\cite{zhu2026tracelab}. Each session is a
     8	sequence of rounds; a round presents a prompt of median 23{,}187 tokens
     9	(p90 51{,}064; maximum 89{,}436, which alone is 88\% of the KV pool), and
    10	between rounds the session waits on a tool before returning with a longer
    11	context. Arrivals are Poisson at $\lambda=0.04\,\text{s}^{-1}$ over an
    12	80-minute window, giving 999 steps in total. Two sessions of maximum size
    13	cannot be co-resident: their combined contexts (89{,}436 and 87{,}821 tokens)
    14	exceed the pool by 75\%.
    15	
    16	The tool wait is the load's defining property and the one most easily
    17	misread. Its \emph{median} is 0.35\,s, which invites the conclusion that
    18	sessions never leave the engine. Its \emph{mean} is 16.85\,s measured and 15.82\,s
    19	demanded, and the maximum is 300\,s; 21.8\% of the
    20	tool waits the trace specifies exceed ten seconds---18.0\% of the 799 round
    21	transitions, 14.4\% of its 999 steps---and 18.6\% of the measured gaps do. We verified that our replay
    22	harness honors the trace faithfully: across 799 round transitions the measured
    23	gap is 16.85\,s mean against a demanded 15.82\,s. On a typical transition the
    24	harness adds nothing---the median of the per-transition measured/demanded
    25	ratios is 1.001---while the ratio of the two means is 1.065, the difference
    26	sitting in the tail. The corpus's own characterization makes the same point
    27	qualitatively---it reports
    28	``diverse and heavily-tailed tool calls'' and calls these gaps
    29	``human-paced''---but gives no numeric mean \cite{zhu2026tracelab}, so the
    30	16.85\,s above is our measurement over the released trace, not a figure we
    31	quote from that paper.
    32	
    33	\subsection{Engine and the binding constraint}
    34	
    35	We use SGLang v0.5.10 \cite{zheng2024sglang} with Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct
    36	\cite{qwen2coder} extended to 96K context,
    37	on one RTX 3090 (24\,GB). KV is paged \cite{kwon2023pagedattention}; at \texttt{mem-fraction-static} 0.85
    38	the pool is 101{,}432 tokens. Hierarchical caching (HiCache), with a host tier of twice the device pool, is
    39	enabled on the configurations marked \emph{+HC} and on every \sys{} run; the
    40	five plain static caps run without it. We keep both because the host tier turns
    41	out to change the mechanism (\S\ref{sec:model}). Every run is guarded: if the pool does not
    42	come out at $101{,}432 \pm 20$---which happens when another tenant holds
    43	memory on the card---the run is aborted rather than recorded, because SGLang
    44	sizes the pool from free memory at startup and a shrunken pool silently
    45	produces incomparable numbers.
    46	
    47	The relevant consequence is that the pool holds only about four median
    48	sessions. Concurrency is therefore capacity-bound, not scheduler-bound, and
    49	this is the premise on which everything below rests.
    50	
    51	\subsection{The controller under test}
    52	
    53	\sys{} is an admission controller that reads engine telemetry and issues
    54	\emph{named} permits rather than a scalar cap: a session is admitted by
    55	appearing in a permit file, and the runner refuses to start a round for a
    56	session that is not listed. This design choice matters for the comparison,
    57	because it is what allows per-session actions at all---including the pause
    58	primitive we exploit in \S\ref{sec:results:gate}. It also means that a session,
    59	once admitted, holds its permit for its entire lifetime, so the controller
    60	never frees capacity by discarding a session's state---it admits fewer sessions
    61	instead. That is a statement about the \emph{policy}, not about the memory: the
    62	engine's radix cache still evicts parked prefixes under pressure, and we
    63	measure that it does and that they are recomputed (\S\ref{sec:model}). We
    64	return to this distinction in \S\ref{sec:discussion}, because it bounds how far
    65	our negative result generalises.
    66	
    67	Each control cycle (2\,s) the controller computes
    68	\begin{equation}
    69	  \Pi \;=\; \max(\rho, \textstyle\sum_i c_i) \;+\; \textstyle\sum_i g_i ,
    70	  \label{eq:proj}
    71	\end{equation}
    72	where $\rho$ is the engine's reported occupancy, $c_i$ the context length of
    73	live session $i$, and $g_i$ its forecast growth over the next
    74	\texttt{horizon} rounds. A session is admitted when $\Pi + n \le B$, with $n$
    75	the candidate's estimated need and $B$ the budget. The three terms are
    76	respectively the measured physical occupancy, a reservation for sessions
    77	currently parked in a tool wait (whose KV is evictable and therefore absent
    78	from $\rho$), and a reservation for future growth.
    79	
    80	\subsection{Baselines and the verdict criterion}
    81	
    82	Baselines are static caps: at most $N$ sessions resident, $N \in \{1,\dots,5\}$,
    83	with and without HiCache. We compare on two axes: end-to-end wall clock
    84	(time from the first submission to the last completion) and SLO attainment
    85	(fraction of requests whose time-to-first-token is under 10\,s).
    86	
    87	The verdict criterion deserves care, because ``faster than cap-$N$'' is not
    88	the question. The question is whether a policy reaches a point \emph{above}
    89	the static front. A point that lies merely between two static caps is not a
    90	win: mixing cap-$N$ and cap-$M$ by time slice approximates any point on the
    91	line between them. We flag this segment argument as an argument, not a
    92	measurement---we did not run the time-sliced policies, and carrying queue state
    93	and cache contents across a switch could bend the line. It is the criterion we
    94	apply, and it is the stricter of the two we could have used---stricter than
    95	comparing each controller against the discrete static points alone, because the
    96	line sits above them. We state the direction plainly rather than claim it as a
    97	caution: this choice makes our negative result \emph{easier} to reach, not
    98	harder. Our best arm is $0.93$\,pp below the line and would be $+3.7$\,pp
    99	against the nearest static point below it in wall clock (\texttt{cap3}+HiCache's
   100	first run, 81.1\% at 236.4 minutes; $+2.9$ would be the comparison against that
   101	anchor's repeat at 81.9\%, which is the one we displaced it with). So we take the
   102	upper-left
   103	envelope of the static points and report each controller's signed distance
   104	above or below it. We classify a signed distance of more than $+1.0$\,pp as
   105	above the front, less than $-1.0$\,pp as below it, and the band between the
   106	two as \emph{on} it---a tie, not a win. The band is a convention we fixed
   107	before running anything, and we report it here because every verdict in this
   108	paper depends on it. Our later replicate measurements (\S\ref{sec:results})
   109	bracket it rather than justify it: the SLO spread of configurations we ran
   110	more than once ranges from 0.5\,pp to 5.1\,pp, so for some configurations
   111	the band is generous and for others it is far too narrow. We flag this as a
   112	weakness of the criterion, not a defence of it. One further property of the
   113	envelope deserves flagging, because
   114	three of the nine controller variants finish slower than 295 minutes. Its
   115	right-hand segment runs from \texttt{cap2}+HiCache at 295 minutes to
   116	\texttt{cap1} at 527, and \texttt{cap1} is the only anchor there that runs
   117	\emph{without} HiCache. Those three---\texttt{hz2},
   118	\texttt{breshz1} and \texttt{foyfix}---are therefore judged against an
   119	interpolation between one host-tier point and one device-only point. If the
   120	envelope is drawn from the host-tier anchors alone, that segment does not exist
   121	and those three cannot be adjudicated at all; drawn from device-only anchors
   122	alone, they sit further below. We report the conservative reading and note that
   123	the honest statement about those three is \emph{below the front as we defined
   124	it}, not \emph{below every static configuration}. A fourth arm,
   125	\texttt{bres}, sat in the same position in an earlier version of this
   126	paper; its re-measured value is 265.9 minutes, which puts it back inside the
   127	device-only range, so the class has three members and not four. Two of the
   128	three
   129	(\texttt{hz2}, \texttt{breshz1}) were also measured under host
   130	co-tenancy and are marked $^\ddagger$ in Table~\ref{tab:runs}; whether they
   131	are slower than 295 minutes at all is one of the things the re-runs will
   132	settle. The front and every
   133	controller's signed distance are computed by one script from the raw per-step
   134	logs; the table generator computes the distance for the four runs that did not
   135	complete all 999 steps---both are named in \S\ref{sec:repro}---and that
   136	script's own first version was wrong: it extrapolated past the end of the
   137	envelope and declared the slowest static cap a winner. We fixed it and report
   138	the episode because it is the failure mode this criterion is most prone to.

     1	\section{A Criterion for When Admission Control Can Pay}
     2	\label{sec:criterion}
     3	
     4	The decomposition turns the
     5	question into one number. Throughput during busy time is
     6	$\overline{R}/\tau$, so raising concurrency by a factor $f$ buys a factor
     7	$f^{1-\epsilon}$, where
     8	\begin{equation}
     9	  \epsilon \;=\; \frac{\mathrm{d}\ln \tau}{\mathrm{d}\ln \overline{R}}
    10	  \label{eq:elasticity}
    11	\end{equation}
    12	is the elasticity of per-step cost with respect to the running count. At
    13	$\epsilon = 0$ the step cost is flat, concurrency converts one for one into
    14	throughput, and an admission controller has room to work. At $\epsilon = 1$
    15	the two cancel exactly and it has none. Everything in between is a
    16	quantitative statement about how much room.
    17	
    18	On the five device-only configurations, where \S\ref{sec:model} establishes
    19	that the step cost is driven by recomputation, $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$
    20	runs from 1.00 to 1.84. A least-squares fit of Eq.~\ref{eq:elasticity} over
    21	those five points gives $\epsilon = 0.61$ on the per-request measurement
    22	($R^2 = 0.89$), or $0.38$ on the model-consistent one (Fig.~\ref{fig:cancel}).
    23	
    24	That fit is a straight line through a curve, and the curve is what matters. The
    25	segment elasticities are $\epsilon = -0.21$ from \texttt{cap1} to
    26	\texttt{cap2}, $+0.43$ from \texttt{cap2} to \texttt{cap3}, $+1.15$ from
    27	\texttt{cap3} to \texttt{cap4}, and $+1.14$ from \texttt{cap4} to
    28	\texttt{cap5}. Below $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}} \approx 1.5$ added
    29	concurrency still buys something; above it, $\epsilon$ passes one, which means
    30	the engine gets \emph{slower} in wall clock as more requests run. The single
    31	number $0.38$ averages those regimes and describes none of them.
    32	
    33	The operating region is the one that matters, and it is not the region the
    34	controllers run in. All nine controller arms run with HiCache enabled, which
    35	the five device-only caps above do not. Measuring the same elasticity on the
    36	three HiCache configurations we have gives $\epsilon = +0.19$ from
    37	\texttt{cap2}+HC to \texttt{cap3}+HC and $+0.46$ from \texttt{cap3}+HC to
    38	\texttt{cap4}+HC, over $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.25 to 1.73. That range
    39	covers the whole controller operating region (1.35 to 1.69), and over it
    40	$\epsilon$ never approaches one. At matched running counts the HiCache caps pay
    41	$1.7$\,ms less per step at $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}} \approx 1.5$.
    42	
    43	This contradicts what an earlier draft of this paper claimed, and it changes
    44	what the criterion is for. The cliff in the device-only curve is a
    45	recomputation effect: \S\ref{sec:model} measures that those runs recompute what
    46	they evict, while under HiCache the host tier absorbs it. The cliff does not
    47	survive on the configuration our controllers actually use. So the criterion
    48	does \emph{not} explain why they lose---on their own configuration they were
    49	not in a region where added concurrency was self-defeating, and the engine had
    50	room they did not take. What the measurement does is rule one explanation out.
    51	The failure is not that this workload denies admission control its lever; it is
    52	that our controllers do not pull the lever they have, which is what
    53	\S\ref{sec:results:idle-memory} and the attribution of
    54	\S\ref{sec:results:attribution} measure directly.
    55	
    56	We are explicit about what this does and does not establish. The HiCache curve
    57	rests on three configurations and two segments, one run each, with no
    58	error bars, and it stops at 1.73---we cannot see whether a cliff appears
    59	beyond the controllers' region. A $\pm10\%$ perturbation of $\tau$ puts the
    60	segment elasticities' bounds across one even on the device-only curve, so the
    61	cliff is a description of the fitted points rather than a threshold we have
    62	located precisely. And it is measured on
    63	different policies rather than by an intervention, so it is a
    64	cross-policy association. On our data it is also computed from the same wall
    65	clock the model then explains---$\tau_1$ is derived from $W_{\text{busy}}$, so
    66	using it to predict $W_{\text{busy}}$ restates rather than tests---and its
    67	points are different policies, so the slope is a cross-policy association, not
    68	a transportable causal derivative. $\tau_2$ is genuinely independent of the
    69	wall clock but measures only decode, so it cannot be dropped into a throughput
    70	equation that includes prefill.
    71	
    72	We tested whether the curve transfers to a second workload. On a real agent
    73	trace (117 sessions, median inter-round
    74	waits of 20.2\,s against 0.34\,s on the synthetic trace,
    75	\texttt{toolwait.csv}, six static caps and one controller arm) the
    76	model-consistent elasticity is negative over the first two segments---$-2.40$
    77	then $-0.28$, at $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.08 to 1.29---and then turns
    78	positive above 1.29 ($+1.0$, $+0.18$, $+0.87$ over the three segments that
    79	reach 1.50). The $\varepsilon > 1$ regime is touched and not held there: the
    80	first of those three segments is the only one at or above one, and a $\pm10\%$
    81	perturbation of $\tau_1$ carries every one of their bounds across both zero and
    82	one. The main trace, for contrast, crosses one at 1.53 with $+1.15$ and stays
    83	above it. So the direction transfers and the location does not, and on this
    84	trace we can say no more than that added concurrency stopped paying. The
    85	per-request measure is no steadier on
    86	that trace ($+0.41$, $+0.48$, $+0.45$, $-1.25$, $+0.73$). The controller arm
    87	there runs at 1.22, below every one of the nine arms above and below where the
    88	branch turns expensive on either trace, and still does not beat the static
    89	front at the threshold this paper uses---though at a stricter one it does
    90	(\S\ref{sec:xtrace}). We report this as a boundary:
    91	the criterion identifies a branch, and where we could check it elsewhere the
    92	sign of the branch transferred while its location did not; it never explained
    93	the failure. It is not, as an earlier draft claimed, something that decides the
    94	question in minutes before building anything.
