     1	\section{Decomposing the Wall Clock}
     2	\label{sec:model}
     3	
     4	\subsection{Two components}
     5	
     6	We split the wall clock at every sampled instant according to whether the
     7	engine has any request in flight:
     8	\begin{equation}
     9	  W \;=\; W_{\text{busy}} \;+\; W_{\text{idle}} .
    10	  \label{eq:split}
    11	\end{equation}
    12	Fig.~\ref{fig:decomp} shows the split for the twenty-eight runs of
    13	Table~\ref{tab:runs}'s families that we have per-step logs for. Two things are
    14	immediately visible and both matter later.
    15	
    16	First, idle time is not noise. Across the runs it spans 7.3\% to 41.9\% of
    17	the sampled span---7.3\% for the fastest configuration, 36.3\% for the
    18	slowest, and 41.9\% for the one that idles most (all-throttles-off). The
    19	denominator here
    20	is the span the sampler observed, which runs about six minutes longer than the
    21	request wall clock of Table~\ref{tab:runs}, so the fractions against that
    22	wall clock are 7.6\% and 36.7\%---and it is not spread evenly.
    23	Over 1{,}844 stretches the mean is 20.9\,s and 17.5\% of them exceed
    24	20\,s, accounting for 69.9\% of all idle \emph{time}
    25	(Fig.~\ref{fig:idle}). The tail, not the middle, is what a scheduler feels,
    26	and it sits at the scale of the 15.8--16.9\,s mean tool wait (demanded and
    27	measured; \texttt{toolwait.csv}). We report the mean and the share rather
    28	than the median here because the median is not robust to which runs enter the
    29	pool: it is 10.0\,s without \texttt{cap1} and 5.0--5.5\,s with it, since
    30	that one run contributes 411 of the 1{,}844 stretches and is itself the
    31	longest. The mean (18.8--20.9\,s) and the over-20\,s share of idle time
    32	(64.6--70.1\%) move by little across the four poolings we report in
    33	\texttt{idle\_pooled.csv}, so the claim that the tail dominates does not
    34	depend on the choice. The 90th percentile moves with the median
    35	(25.1--30.1\,s) and we give it as a range for the same reason.
    36	
    37	Second, the two components move independently, and which way idle moves tells
    38	you nothing about which way the wall clock moves. The no-control baseline
    39	(\S\ref{sec:results}) cuts idle time by 13.8 minutes against the base
    40	configuration---it admits everything, so nothing queues---and finishes only
    41	2.7 minutes sooner, because its busy time grows by 11.1 minutes; the trade
    42	costs it the SLO column outright, 89.8\% down to 3.0\%. The
    43	no-gate-no-reservation arm moves the other way: idle \emph{rises} by 10.1
    44	minutes while busy falls by 29.2, for a 19.0-minute gain. Any account that
    45	treats ``more concurrency'' as one thing will get this wrong in one direction
    46	or the other. Neither example is one of the damaged runs of
    47	\S\ref{sec:repro}: the baseline is clean and the no-gate arm carries the
    48	weaker \emph{watch} flag, three lanes for over an hour with no measured
    49	inflation. The charge-free base arm is a third case, and we give its split in
    50	\S\ref{sec:results} with the re-measured numbers.
    51	
    52	\begin{figure}[tb]
    53	  \centering
    54	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig2_decomp.pdf}
    55	  \caption{Wall clock split into busy and idle for the twenty-eight runs of
    56	  \S\ref{sec:model} and \S\ref{sec:results}, ordered by total duration. Idle is 7.3\% for the fastest configuration and
    57	  36.3\% for the slowest. Note the no-control baseline: it sits sixth of
    58	  twenty-eight on idle share (11.4\%) and yet sixteenth on duration, because
    59	  its busy time grows with everything it admits. Five bars are
    60	  hatched---their busy time is inflated by host
    61	  co-tenancy (\S\ref{sec:repro}); the hatch is the same $^\ddagger$ flag as in
    62	  Table~\ref{tab:runs}.}
    63	  \label{fig:decomp}
    64	\end{figure}
    65	
    66	\begin{figure}[tb]
    67	  \centering
    68	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig4_idle_segments.pdf}
    69	  \caption{Survival function of idle-stretch duration, pooled over all
    70	  completed runs (1{,}844 stretches). Most stretches are short---the median is
    71	  5--10\,s depending on which runs enter the pool, which is why we do not lead
    72	  with it---but the minutes are not: 69.9\% of all idle \emph{time} sits in
    73	  stretches longer than 20\,s (64.6--70.1\% across the four poolings in
    74	  \texttt{idle\_pooled.csv}), and the mean stretch is 20.9\,s, the same order
    75	  as the 15.8--16.9\,s mean tool wait. The cliff is the 5\,s sampling floor.}
    76	  \label{fig:idle}
    77	\end{figure}
    78	
    79	\subsection{What determines busy time}
    80	
    81	During busy time the engine executes steps; each step produces one token per
    82	running request. Hence
    83	\begin{equation}
    84	  W_{\text{busy}} \;\approx\; \frac{G}{\overline{R}}\,\tau ,
    85	  \label{eq:busy}
    86	\end{equation}
    87	with $G$ the total generated tokens (fixed by the trace at 706{,}010) and
    88	$\tau$ the per-step cost. Across our runs $\tau$ is not a fit parameter we
    89	tuned; we obtain it two independent ways, and reporting both is what shows
    90	they are measuring different things rather than confirming each other.
    91	
    92	\paragraph{Method 1 (derived).} $\tau_1 = W_{\text{busy}} \cdot \overline{R} /
    93	G$. Two choices in that expression are easy to get wrong and both matter. The
    94	time term must be the \emph{busy} component, not the wall clock---using the
    95	wall clock charges idle time to the steps. And $\overline{R}$ must be the mean
    96	running count \emph{during busy time}, not the mean over the whole run: the
    97	whole-run mean includes the idle samples, where the count is zero, so it equals
    98	$\overline{R}_{\text{busy}} \times$ (busy fraction). Using it makes $\tau_1$
    99	too low by exactly the busy fraction---so the estimate is short by the
   100	\emph{idle} fraction, 36.3\% for \texttt{cap1}, whose busy share is 63.7\%. We report both columns in the data, and use the
   101	busy-conditional mean throughout; the whole-run mean is what an earlier
   102	revision of this paper used, and it made the two methods agree far better than
   103	they do.
   104	
   105	\paragraph{Method 2 (independent).} For each step record we compute the
   106	per-request decode interval,
   107	$(\text{total\_duration} - \text{first\_token}) / (\text{output\_len} - 1)$,
   108	and take the median over steps with $\text{output\_len} \ge 32$.
   109	
   110	The two do not agree exactly, and the disagreement is informative rather than
   111	embarrassing. $\tau_1$ exceeds $\tau_2$ on every run we accept, by 7.7--39.7\%
   112	(\texttt{decomp.csv}, column \texttt{excess\_pct}), which in
   113	absolute terms is 2.6--8.2\,ms of a 27--37\,ms step. (The one run we exclude
   114	from the reuse accounting, the \texttt{horizon}=1 repeat, reads 66.3\% here
   115	against its original's 18.5\%---a second engine-side counter that is out of
   116	line with its own configuration, which is why we treat that run's numbers as
   117	unusable rather than as a measurement we dislike.) The gap is
   118	\emph{consistent with} engine steps that are prefill and
   119	therefore emit no output token---we can bound it, not identify it, because
   120	subtracting a median from a mean does not isolate a component. All we can form
   121	from these two is their ratio: the excess $\tau_1/\tau_2 - 1$ runs
   122	17.7--39.7\% across the five device-only configurations. Two things follow, and
   123	we separate them. First, it is a ratio of \emph{times}, not of steps, and we
   124	have no measurement of the fraction of step \emph{counts} that are prefill---
   125	chunked prefill makes those two different numbers. Second, even as a time
   126	ratio it is the gap between two measurements, not a decomposition of one: it
   127	is consistent with prefill and non-decode work filling it, but we cannot rule
   128	out that some of the difference is the two estimators disagreeing for other
   129	reasons. So the two are not
   130	two estimates of one number; they are measurements of two different things,
   131	and we do not size a component with their difference. Fig.~\ref{fig:steptime} shows both, run by run. Method 2 remains the one that does not depend on the
   132	quantity it is used to explain, and it is the one we quote when the question
   133	is the cost of a decode step. The engine's own latency counters
   134	(\texttt{itl\_p50}, \texttt{e2e\_p50}, \texttt{ttft\_p50}) are exported as zero
   135	by SGLang v0.5.10 in our configuration, so no directly measured step time
   136	exists in our data; instrumenting this is future work.
   137	
   138	\begin{figure}[tb]
   139	  \centering
   140	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig3_steptime.pdf}
   141	  \caption{Per-step cost by two independent measurements, one run per row:
   142	  circles are model-consistent (all engine steps), crosses are the per-request
   143	  decode interval. They do not agree---$\tau_1$ exceeds $\tau_2$ by 8--40\%---
   144	  and the gap is consistent with the prefill share, not measurement error;
   145	  \S\ref{sec:model} bounds that share rather than identifying it. The dotted line is
   146	  the cost of reading the weights once; every run sits above it. The six rows
   147	  marked $^\ddagger$ were measured under host co-tenancy, which is why they
   148	  cluster at the high end---their step times are the inflated ones
   149	  (\S\ref{sec:repro}).}
   150	  \label{fig:steptime}
   151	\end{figure}
   152	
   153	\subsection{The per-step cost is not constant in concurrency}
   154	
   155	Equation~\ref{eq:busy} would be uninteresting if $\tau$ were a constant. It is
   156	not. Across the thirteen runs we fitted---the five device-only caps, the three
   157	host-tier caps that span the same range, and the five controller arms we report
   158	individually---$\tau_1$ ranges from 27.0 to 36.7\,ms and $\tau_2$
   159	from 20.6 to 33.1\,ms, and both rise with the running count: fitting
   160	Eq.~\ref{eq:elasticity} to the five device-only configurations gives
   161	$\varepsilon = 0.38$ for $\tau_1$ and $0.61$ for $\tau_2$
   162	($R^2 = 0.89$ for the latter, Fig.~\ref{fig:cancel}).
   163	
   164	The relation is a trend, not a law. Two host-tier configurations with similar
   165	running counts behave differently: \texttt{cap4\_hc} runs 1.73 requests at
   166	29.8\,ms while \texttt{F:hz0} runs 1.66 at 35.7\,ms. Running count sets a
   167	floor and a trend, not a value---and on the host-tier family, as we show below,
   168	it does not set the value at all.
   169	
   170	This is the crux of the paper, and we state it at the strength the arithmetic
   171	supports. Hold $\tau_1$ at its value for \texttt{cap1}, 28.8\,ms, and busy
   172	time falls as $1/\overline{R}$: raising the running count from 1.00 to 1.84
   173	should have cut busy time by a factor of 1.84, from 340 minutes to 184. The
   174	measured curve flattens and lands at 234. The rise in $\tau$ takes back 50 of
   175	the 156 minutes that the extra concurrency would otherwise have bought---\emph{a
   176	third} of the gain, not all of it and, as an earlier revision of this paper
   177	claimed, not more than half. The other two thirds are real: raising
   178	concurrency does make the engine faster. What it does not do is make it faster
   179	in proportion, and the shortfall compounds with every controller that tries to
   180	exploit the difference.
   181	
   182	\paragraph{What the engine is doing when it is not decoding.} SGLang exposes
   183	two counters that answer this directly: tokens evicted from the radix cache,
   184	and prompt tokens that had to be prefilled rather than reused. Across the five
   185	static caps \emph{without} HiCache the two track each other almost exactly.
   186	\texttt{cap1} evicts 9.2\,M tokens and prefills 8.6\,M uncached;
   187	\texttt{cap5} evicts 21.3\,M and prefills 20.7\,M; the gap is 0.6--0.7\,M on
   188	every one of the five. An evicted prefix is recomputed, close to one token for
   189	one, and that recomputation is chunked into decode steps. Those same five runs
   190	give recomputation rising with $\tau$ across four of the five points: 9.2\,M
   191	uncached tokens at 27.4\,ms, 11.5 at 29.7, 15.4 at 32.4, 20.7 at 36.7. The
   192	exception is the first point, \texttt{cap1}, which recomputes least (8.6\,M)
   193	and still costs 28.8\,ms---more than \texttt{cap2} does for more recomputation.
   194	We report the ordering we have rather than rounding it to a monotone one: it is
   195	a mechanism consistent with the $\tau$ trend on four of the five device-only
   196	configurations, and one point it does not order. It is five points on five
   197	different policies, not an
   198	intervention: we did not hold concurrency fixed and vary eviction, so we call
   199	it consistent rather than demonstrated.
   200	
   201	\begin{figure}[tb]
   202	  \centering
   203	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig7_elasticity.pdf}
   204	  \caption{Per-step cost against the running count during busy time. Solid
   205	  circles are $\tau_1$ and squares $\tau_2$ for the five device-only
   206	  configurations; $\tau_1$ is model-consistent (it averages over all engine
   207	  steps, including prefill that emits no output token), $\tau_2$ is the
   208	  per-request decode interval, and $\tau_1$ exceeds $\tau_2$ by 8--40\%. The
   209	  green triangles are $\tau_1$ for the three HiCache configurations---the ones
   210	  the controllers run---and they are drawn against the same axes to make
   211	  \S\ref{sec:criterion}'s point visible: over $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.25
   212	  to 1.73, which covers the whole controller operating range, the host-tier
   213	  curve rises 27.0 to 29.8\,ms while the device-only curve over a comparable
   214	  span rises 27.4 to 36.7. We plot $\tau_1$ only for the HiCache points to keep
   215	  the figure readable. The fitted exponent is the elasticity $\varepsilon$ of
   216	  Eq.~\ref{eq:elasticity}; it is fit to the device-only points alone.}
   217	  \label{fig:cancel}
   218	\end{figure}
   219	
   220	\paragraph{Where the mechanism stops.} With HiCache enabled the
   221	correspondence breaks---and it breaks in a way that confirms the mechanism
   222	rather than undermining it. The device cache misses as much as before:
   223	\texttt{cap4\_hc} misses 22.4\,M tokens on the device, more than the
   224	device-only \texttt{cap4} misses. But only 9.1\,M of that is recomputed; the
   225	other 13.3\,M is served from the host tier. Across the nineteen host-tier
   226	runs the host serves 9.5--19.3\,M tokens each, and across the five device-only
   227	runs it serves almost nothing---0.006\% to 0.25\% of the device miss, the rest
   228	of which is recomputed on all five. Recomputation is still what the device cannot
   229	absorb; the host tier is a second absorber in front of it.
   230

   231	\subsection{The tier, not the policy, decides whether reuse survives}
   232	
   233	The device-miss column hides the quantity that matters. Define
   234	\emph{reuse} as the prompt tokens served from anywhere in the memory
   235	hierarchy---device radix cache plus host tier---so that
   236	$\text{reuse} + \text{recomputed} = \text{prompt}$, with the prompt fixed at
   237	28.5\,M by the trace.
   238	
   239	The bound on reuse has to be defined independently of what we observed,
   240	or the argument is circular. We take it from the trace: summing the planned
   241	reusable prefix (\texttt{prefix\_len}) over all 999 rounds of
   242	\texttt{replay\_pois200\_l04.csv} gives 21{,}540{,}371 tokens against a prompt
   243	total of 28{,}526{,}871, so no policy can reuse more than 75.5\% of the
   244	prompt. That is a property of the workload. It is also a bound rather than a
   245	description: \emph{no configuration we ran reaches it}, and the best realizes
   246	92.5\%, so the gap between ``planned'' and ``achieved'' exists even in the
   247	easiest case. What varies across configurations is how much of the bound is
   248	realized, and that is where the tier enters.
   249	
   250	\begin{center}
   251	% 这张表在 conference 模板下比单栏宽 56.9pt（Overfull 会一直挂在编译日志里）。
   252	% \small + tabcolsep 3pt 后 244.4pt < 252pt。改列宽不是改数字。
   253	\small\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}%
   254	\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}
   255	\toprule
   256	device-only & $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ & 1.00 & 1.27 & 1.53 & 1.65 & 1.84 \\
   257	            & realized (\% of bound) & 92.5 & 89.7 & 79.0 & 60.7 & 36.4 \\
   258	            & recomputed (M)         &  8.6 &  9.2 & 11.5 & 15.4 & 20.7 \\
   259	\midrule
   260	host tier   & $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ & 1.25 & 1.51 & 1.73 & & \\
   261	            & realized (\% of bound) & 92.4 & 91.3 & 90.2 & & \\
   262	            & recomputed (M)         &  8.6 &  8.9 &  9.1 & & \\
   263	\bottomrule
   264	\end{tabular}
   265	\end{center}
   266	
   267	Device-only, realization falls from 92.5\% to 36.4\% and the shortfall is
   268	recomputation: 8.6\,M to 20.7\,M tokens. With a host tier it holds at
   269	90.2--92.4\% on the static points that span the same range, and at
   270	72.8--92.5\% across the nineteen host-tier runs that completed this
   271	trace---the same trace, HiCache on, at least 995 of its 999 steps---including
   272	every controller arm; the lowest is the arm that disables every throttle and
   273	therefore evicts the most (21.1\,M tokens, the largest in the table). That rule
   274	on its own selects \emph{twenty}, and we name the four runs it is not meant to
   275	catch, because a reader scanning the logs will find them: two that stopped at
   276	763 and 770 steps (they realize 70.7\% and 67.1\%, and we exclude them for not
   277	finishing rather than for their values), the variable-load family (a different
   278	trace, and short of the step count as well), and one whose engine
   279	prompt-token counter reads $1.41\times$ the trace plan with zero retractions
   280	and no client-side retries, which we could not reconcile.
   281	while the \emph{device} misses more than the device-only family's at every
   282	point (21.6--27.9\,M against 8.6--20.7\,M), because the host serves
   283	9.5--19.3\,M in front of the recomputation instead of letting the device keep
   284	it. A reader watching only the device hit rate would conclude the host tier
   285	made things worse. (Two denominators appear here and we name them: the 75.5\%
   286	is the trace's bound as a share of the prompt, and 92.5\% is the best observed
   287	reuse as a share of \emph{that bound}---\texttt{cap1}, the run that realizes
   288	it, reuses 69.9\% of its prompt. \texttt{reuse.csv} carries \texttt{reuse\_frac} and
   289	\texttt{realized\_frac} side by side so the two cannot be confused.)
   290	
   291	This is the same collapse \S\ref{sec:related} reports under a different
   292	normalization. That section quotes the round-1-and-later prefix hit rate---%
   293	steps restricted to rounds $\ge 1$, unweighted mean over steps---which falls
   294	from 80.8\% to 32.8\% across the device-only caps and holds at 80.7\% to
   295	78.7\% with a host tier. The two conventions differ in two ways, not one:
   296	which steps are in the set (round $\ge 1$ against all rounds) and how steps
   297	are combined (unweighted mean against token-weighted sum). We use the
   298	token-weighted all-steps convention here because reuse and recomputation must
   299	add to the prompt, and we name the other whenever we quote it. Both are
   300	\emph{realized} quantities that vary with the policy; neither is the trace's
   301	planned rate, which is fixed at 75.5\% and appears in this section only as
   302	the bound.
   303	
   304	Device occupancy does not explain the split. \texttt{cap3\_fixed} and
   305	\texttt{cap3\_hc} run at nearly the same mean device occupancy (55.8\% and
   306	55.5\%) and nearly the same running count (1.53 and 1.51), and they realize
   307	79.0\% and 91.3\% of the bound respectively. What differs is not how full the
   308	device pool is but what happens when it overflows: with no host tier an
   309	evicted prefix is gone and must be recomputed; with one it is demoted and read
   310	back. The pool is the binding resource, the tier sets the pool, and the tier
   311	therefore sets whether added concurrency is paid for in recomputation. The
   312	caveat is that this pair is two runs on two different days and two different
   313	GPUs, not a paired experiment; \S\ref{sec:repro} states what we did to rule
   314	out co-tenancy. A capacity-controlled version---same card, same trace, only
   315	the pool size changed---is queued behind the re-runs and we report it in
   316	\S\ref{sec:results} when it lands; we do not use it here.
   317	
   318	\paragraph{The second trace agrees on direction and not on level.}
   319	We ran the same accounting on a second workload, reported in full in
   320	\S\ref{sec:xtrace}---117 real Claude agent sessions, 390 rounds, the same pool
   321	and the same engine---and it reproduces the direction: its realization of \emph{its own} bound falls from 74.8\% to 57.5\%
   322	as the running count rises from 1.08 to 1.50, and over that span the whole-range
   323	per-unit slope is $-40.9$ points, against the device-only family's $-66.8$ over
   324	$\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.00--1.84 (\texttt{slope.csv}). What it does not reproduce is the level, and this is
   325	worth stating because it bounds what we can claim. The lightest cap on that
   326	trace (\texttt{realhr\_cap2}) runs at a \emph{lower} median device occupancy
   327	than the lightest on this one (15.8\% against 22.4\%, \texttt{cap1}) and
   328	realizes \emph{less} (74.8\% against 92.5\%), so realization is not a function
   329	of occupancy alone---and a
   330	statement of the form ``the pool collapses above $x$\% occupancy'' is wrong on
   331	our own data. Occupancy is instantaneous while reuse depends on history; a
   332	session's prefix must survive from one round to the next, and how much
   333	pressure that puts on the pool is not visible in what the pool holds at an
   334	instant. We do not have the measurement that would separate those, which is
   335	why the controlled experiment we report in \S\ref{sec:results} varies the
   336	pool alone, on one card and one trace, rather than leaning on the cross-trace
   337	comparison. The claim this section supports is the narrow one: within a
   338	configuration, the host tier preserves realization at a matched running count
   339	and a matched occupancy, and without it realization falls.
   340	
   341	That gives the criterion of \S\ref{sec:criterion} a mechanism rather than a
   342	correlation, and it suggests a prediction we are testing:
   343	if the tier acts by multiplying usable capacity (the host pool is twice the
   344	device pool, so three times in total), then shrinking the device pool alone
   345	should move the collapse point even with no host tier involved. We do not
   346	treat the arithmetic as a result---it assumes KV demand grows linearly in the
   347	running count, which heterogeneous sessions need not satisfy---and the
   348	queued experiment measures the collapse point directly rather than predicting
   349	it, and \S\ref{sec:results} reports it once it has run.
   350	
   351	\paragraph{What is left unexplained.} Less than an earlier draft of this
   352	paragraph claimed. Across the nineteen host-tier runs recomputation spans
   353	8.6--12.8\,M tokens and realization 72.8--92.5\%, and $\tau_1$ spans
   354	27.0--36.2\,ms. Three of those runs
   355	(\texttt{F:hz2} 33.6, \texttt{F:hz0} 35.7,
   356	\texttt{F:breshz1} 36.2\,ms) were
   357	measured while three other engine instances shared the host, which inflates
   358	per-step cost by 25--40\% and leaves the volume columns untouched
   359	(\S\ref{sec:repro}); they are being re-measured and we do not use their
   360	timing. A fourth was re-measured clean and is in the set above with its new
   361	value. Across the remaining sixteen the $\tau_1$ spread does not sit
   362	unexplained at all: it tracks their recomputation closely (a straight line
   363	through the sixteen leaves every run within $1.5$\,ms, $r = +0.92$), which is
   364	the mechanism of this section holding inside the host tier rather than a
   365	residual. We report the association and not the line: the coefficients are
   366	fitted to one configuration family and we have no derivation for them. We note that the negative
   367	result does not rest on it either way: the cancellation is measured directly,
   368	not inferred from the mechanism.
   369	
   370	One caution about the strength of the account above, since the arithmetic
   371	invites overreading. $\text{reuse} + \text{recomputed} = \text{prompt}$ is a
   372	definition, not a finding, so ``the shortfall reappears as recomputation''
   373	follows from how we partitioned the prompt and cannot by itself establish the
   374	mechanism. What is independent of the partition is the pair of engine
   375	counters: evicted tokens and uncached prefill tokens are separate SGLang
   376	counters, and on the device-only runs they track within 0.6--0.7\,M on all
   377	five---which is the observation that licenses reading eviction as the cause
   378	of recomputation. With a host tier the two come apart by design, and the
   379	1.3--8.6\,M gap between them is an upper bound on what the host absorbed out
   380	of the evicted pool: those tokens were evicted and not recomputed, which is
   381	consistent with the host having served them and equally consistent with their
   382	never having been asked for again. We can bound it, not identify it
   383	(\texttt{eviction.csv}); the host serves 9.5--19.3\,M in total, so most of what
   384	it serves are prefixes that were still resident on the device when the
   385	session came back for them.
   386	
   387	\paragraph{A refutation we had to withdraw.} An earlier revision withdrew this
   388	mechanism outright, on two checks that were both wrong. The first was that
   389	total prefilled tokens equalled the trace's planned prefill to a ratio of 1.00,
   390	so no token could have been computed twice. \texttt{prompt\_tokens\_total}
   391	counts the full submitted prompt \emph{including} its cached portion, so it
   392	equals the sum of prompt lengths by construction and cannot detect
   393	recomputation; the quantity that can is the uncached count used above, which
   394	varies by 2.4$\times$ across these runs. The second was that SGLang reported
   395	no retractions. Radix-cache eviction is not a retraction---retractions count
   396	running requests preempted mid-flight, a different event with its own counter.
   397	We record the episode because it is the same failure mode as the segment
   398	argument of \S\ref{sec:bg}: a check that looks like a measurement but is an
   399	identity.
   400	
   401	\subsection{Why idle time exists, and why recovering it does not pay}
   402	
   403	The length of an individual idle stretch is a property of the workload, not of
   404	the controller. A session in a
   405	tool wait holds its permit and its KV but issues no request; with only about
   406	two live sessions, the probability that all of them are simultaneously waiting
   407	is substantial. The direct evidence is the duration of the stretches
   408	themselves: they are of the same order as the tool waits, and an order of
   409	magnitude longer than the 2\,s control cycle (Fig.~\ref{fig:idle}). No
   410	controller can shorten a gap that the tool set.
   411	
   412	How much idle a \emph{run} accumulates is a different matter, and there the
   413	controller is not off the hook. Idle accumulates when every live session
   414	happens to be parked at once, and how many sessions are live is precisely what
   415	an admission policy sets. Two configurations with the same mean running count
   416	make this visible: \texttt{horizon}=1 (1.52) and \texttt{cap3}+HiCache (1.51)
   417	idle 36.7 and 24.1 minutes respectively---a 52\% difference at matched
   418	concurrency---and 12.6 of the 19.4-minute wall-clock gap between them is that
   419	idle difference rather than step cost. So the gap durations are the workload's,
   420	and the number of gaps is partly the policy's.
   421	
   422	We note one claim we had to drop here. An earlier draft reported that idle
   423	fraction falls monotonically with mean concurrency. With the full data it does
   424	not: on the busy-conditional measure this section uses throughout,
   425	\texttt{F:hz0} at $\overline{R}_{\text{busy}}$ 1.67 idles 15.2\% while
   426	\texttt{cap4} at 1.65 idles 10.4\%---more concurrency and \emph{more} idle,
   427	the reverse of the reported trend. (The two arms read 1.41 and 1.48 on the
   428	all-samples mean, where the ordering happens to run the other way;
   429	\texttt{arm\_running.csv} carries both conventions side by side, and we name
   430	the one each claim uses.) The weaker statement that survives is the one we use---%
   431	the stretches are structural and long.
   432	
   433	The obvious remedy---keep more sessions live so that someone is always
   434	ready---is what \texttt{bres} does. It works in the narrow sense: \texttt{bres}
   435	recovers about 17 of the minutes that its own baseline spends idle. It is
   436	still slower overall, because the sessions it keeps live raise the step cost
   437	by more than the recovered idle is worth---and, as above, that step-cost rise
   438	is measured under co-tenancy and is therefore an upper bound. Idle is not unrecoverable; it is
   439	recoverable at a price that is not worth paying here.
   440	The structural remedy would be to make the engine do work during a wait, but
   441	there is no work to do: the session's next round does not exist until its tool
   442	returns.

     1	\section{Results: Four Routes, One Outcome}
     2	\label{sec:results}
     3	
     4	\subsection{The frontier}
     5	
     6	Fig.~\ref{fig:frontier} places every completed run on the (wall clock, SLO)
     7	plane and draws the upper-left envelope of the static points. Table~\ref{tab:runs}
     8	gives the numbers and each controller's signed distance from that envelope.
     9	
    10	The result is unambiguous. Of nine controller variants, \emph{none} is above
    11	the envelope---not one, on any run, and the pair we repeated most tightly says
    12	so at a resolution the others do not. The closest arm of all is the one with
    13	single-flight admission disabled, at $0.55$ percentage points \emph{below} the
    14	line---inside our $\pm1.0$\,pp tie band, so we record it as \emph{on} the
    15	line rather than below it, but on the below side of it.
    16	
    17	The base configuration is the one whose repeats we can trust, and they are
    18	tight: four runs spanning 0.5\,pp of SLO (89.5\% mean; 279.8, 289.6, 291.3
    19	and 293.2 minutes at 89.5\%, 89.3\%, 89.8\% and 89.6\%---the matched-pair
    20	experiment ran the same configuration twice more, as \texttt{pairA\_foyerhc}
    21	and \texttt{pairB\_foyerhc}), against the 3.3\,pp that separates it from the
    22	single-flight-off arm. So that
    23	difference is several times the noise of its own configuration rather than
    24	inside it, and it is a trade rather than a win: 28 minutes of wall clock bought
    25	with 3.5\,pp of SLO, moving the arm onto the tie band from below it. Not one
    26	configuration in the table gets above the line, and the tightest pair we have
    27	is what makes us willing to say the near misses are misses. A fifth pair, two runs of \texttt{cap3}+HiCache, \emph{is} used, and an
    28	earlier draft of this paper wrongly set it aside saying one of them exceeded
    29	its own cap. That was a different run: the over-cap one is the device-only
    30	\texttt{cap3} repeat (peak in-flight five against a cap of three; at our
    31	300-point in-flight sample grid, 126 samples sit over the cap, a count that
    32	scales with the grid rather than with the run), while both HiCache runs peak at three with none. The
    33	repeat is also both faster and higher on SLO (230.3 minutes, 81.9\% against
    34	236.4 and 81.1\%), so by the same rule that displaced \texttt{cap4}+HiCache's
    35	first run it displaces this one---and moving that anchor is what puts our
    36	best arm below the line rather than on it.\footnote{The repeats are uneven, and
    37	we do not want the tightest pair to stand for all of them. Five configurations
    38	have been run more than once; their SLO spreads are 0.5, 0.8, 1.0, 3.9 and
    39	5.1\,pp (\texttt{replicates.csv}). The two widest belong to the two arms that
    40	decide something---\texttt{horizon}=1 and the leftmost anchor---so where a small
    41	margin matters our repeats are weakest, and where they are strongest (the base
    42	configuration and the \texttt{cap3}+HiCache anchor) the margin is not small. We
    43	report both ends rather than the average.}
    44	
    45	The anchor repeats moved the frontier twice and we report both moves. The
    46	leftmost anchor's second run came out both faster (209.1 against 213.0 minutes)
    47	and higher on SLO (68.5 against 63.4), displacing the first; no controller
    48	verdict moves, because every controller finishes to the right of the next
    49	anchor. The middle anchor's repeat moved it the other way for us: it is also
    50	faster and higher, and taking it moves our best arm from \emph{on} the line to
    51	\emph{on the line, below it}. We report every repeat we ran rather than keeping
    52	the better of each pair, because a frontier whose anchors are single
    53	measurements is the weakness this whole subsection is about---and here it is
    54	the weakness biting.
    55	
    56	The other end of the front deserves its own sentence, because the rest of this
    57	paper is negative and a reader could close it thinking the answer is to do
    58	nothing. The run with \emph{no} admission control---every session admitted the
    59	moment it arrives---finishes in 288.6 minutes at 3.0\% SLO, $87.6$\,pp below
    60	the envelope and the worst SLO we report. Its median
    61	time-to-first-token is 1{,}774 seconds, its prefix cache hit rate collapses from
    62	the planned 75.5\% the runner prints in its end-of-run summary (the all-steps
    63	convention, a different
    64	one from the round-1+ numbers in \S\ref{sec:related}---for the same trace
    65	\texttt{gen\_nocontrol.py} reports 73.8\% on that convention) to 1.6\%, and its segment SLO is 30\% in the first
    66	tenth of the run and exactly zero in each of the nine after that. Uncontrolled
    67	admission does not degrade gracefully; it destroys the cache and then serves
    68	almost nobody. The question this paper addresses is therefore not whether to
    69	admit selectively, but which selective rule to use.
    70	
    71	Two features of the front are worth noting before the negative results.
    72	The envelope is steep between 209 and 230 minutes (0.63\,pp per minute) and
    73	shallow thereafter (0.15\,pp per minute), so the cost of buying SLO by
    74	retreating from the fast end is high. And $W$ is not monotone in concurrency:
    75	five sessions (260.9\,min) is \emph{slower} than four (249.9\,min)---the same
    76	configuration family, no host cache on either side---because the per-step cost
    77	rises faster than the request count does. We return to this
    78	non-monotonicity in \S\ref{sec:model}, where it turns out to be the first
    79	appearance of the mechanism that drives the whole frontier.
    80

   164	
   165	\subsection{The controllers leave memory unused}
   166	\label{sec:results:idle-memory}
   167	
   168	Before the routes, one measurement that reframes all of them. Our controller
   169	keeps every session's permit for its lifetime, so its pool occupancy is a
   170	policy output, not a constraint. It runs at a median of 49.2\% of the pool on
   171	the base configuration, and the median number of sessions queueing for
   172	admission is 66. The configuration that finishes fastest---the repeat of that same static cap
   173	of four, at 209.1 minutes---runs at 71.9\%, and its first run at 70.2\%. Occupancy
   174	and running requests track each other reliably across the arms we have (the two
   175	columns in \texttt{occupancy.csv} rank the twenty-three arms we have almost
   176	identically, Spearman $\rho=0.95$), but
   177	running count does \emph{not} order wall clock: \texttt{horizon}=2 runs 1.25
   178	requests against the base arm's 1.14 (the all-samples mean, the column
   179	\texttt{occupancy.csv} carries; \S\ref{sec:model} uses the busy-conditional
   180	one) and finishes 15 minutes slower. That gap is
   181	what the next subsection is about.
   182	
   183	The controller believes it is nearly full. Its own decision log records the
   184	projection it admits against---the largest of measured occupancy and the summed
   185	contexts of live sessions, plus a reserve for their growth---at a median of
   186	89{,}073 tokens against a budget of 96{,}360, i.e.\ 7.6\% free. Its measured
   187	occupancy at the same instants is 49{,}875 tokens: 48.2\% of that budget, or
   188	50.8\% of the pool, is idle. The
   189	projection is 1.79$\times$ the truth on this configuration. Across the
   190	sixteen controller runs that finished on this trace and logged projections the
   191	ratio runs from 1.32 to 1.90 with a median of 1.78, and from 1.32 to 4.11 with
   192	the same median once the second trace's controller arm is included
   193	(\texttt{projection.csv} records each set, its size and its median; the second
   194	trace is the 4.11). So 1.79 is this arm's value, not a constant of the design,
   195	and we name the set because the range depends on it---over every finished run in
   196	that file, including the flash-crowd and 25-session families, it is 1.01 to
   197	4.11 with a median of 1.58. (Runs still in flight are excluded from both
   198	figures: their controller logs are still growing, and one of them reads 1.95
   199	today.) Medians here are
   200	the upper of the two middle values, the convention \S\ref{sec:results:attribution}
   201	uses throughout. Two terms produce the gap: charging every
   202	live session its full context, when a parked session's KV is evictable and
   203	therefore absent from the engine's count, and the growth reserve, which alone
   204	is 26{,}762 tokens, 28\% of the budget.
   205	
   206	Two things follow, and we are careful about the second.
   207	
   208	The first is that the controllers under-use the pool, and that this is a
   209	property of their estimates rather than of the hardware: the base
   210	configuration holds nearly half the pool idle (48.2\% of its budget, 50.8\% of\

   208	The first is that the controllers under-use the pool, and that this is a
   209	property of their estimates rather than of the hardware: the base
   210	configuration holds nearly half the pool idle (48.2\% of its budget, 50.8\% of\
   211	the pool) with 66 sessions queued, because
   212	it believes it has 7.6\% free when it has 48.2\%. Any account of why it is slow
   213	that does not mention this is incomplete.
   214	
   215	We then asked what actually stops it, by instrumenting the admission decision.
   216	The path is a conjunction: a session is authorized only if the SLO valve is
   217	open, the round gate is not full, and its projected footprint fits what remains
   218	of the budget. Over the base run's 8{,}196 blocked cycles---cycles with a
   219	session waiting and none admitted---the valve accounts for 6.8\%, the round gate
   220	for none, and a projection already above budget for 38.4\%.
   221	
   222	The remaining 54.8\% are cycles where the controller selected no candidate at
   223	all, and here we are explicit about the limit of what we can resolve. The
   224	controller is single-flight by default: it authorizes at most one session per
   225	two-second cycle and waits for that session to begin a round before authorizing
   226	the next. Whether that gate held or whether a candidate was tried and did not
   227	fit is \emph{not distinguishable from our logs}---the controller does not record
   228	it---so we do not attribute the bucket to either. We bounded it by running that arm: the same configuration with single-flight
   229	disabled. Two things came out of it, and the second is the one that matters.
   230	
   231	The bound first. With the gate off, the residual bucket---which can then only
   232	be \emph{candidates that did not fit}, since the gate cannot be holding---falls
   233	from 54.8\% of blocked cycles to 39.7\%, against 44.0\% that are over budget
   234	outright. So the single-flight path accounts for roughly fifteen points, and
   235	the headroom estimate remains the largest single cause. (Both arms' numbers
   236	are rows of \texttt{gate\_attrib.csv}, which now includes the \texttt{sf=0}
   237	arm; an earlier revision quoted 39.9 and 43.5 for the second pair, values that
   238	appeared in no data file.)
   239	We report what did \emph{not} move, because it is the check we wrote down for
   240	this probe before running it. The two aggregate indicators barely did:
   241	admitting cycles go from 2.35\% to 2.44\% and blocked cycles from 93.3\% to
   242	93.2\%, so on those two the flag looks like it changed nothing. What moves is
   243	the composition \emph{within} the blocked cycles---which is the quantity the
   244	bound above rests on---and it moves well outside the replicate spread, the
   245	repeat of the base arm reading 58.3\% remainder against the original's 54.8\%.
   246	So the probe supports the decomposition and not the claim that disabling the
   247	gate makes the controller admit appreciably more, which is what we say above
   248	it buys 28 minutes for 3.5\,pp rather than a win.
   249	
   250	The second thing is that disabling it does not make the controller win.
   251	\texttt{sf=0} finishes in 263.3 minutes at 86.3\% SLO against the base run's
   252	291.3 and 89.8\%: it buys 28 minutes for 3.5\,pp, and its signed distance from
   253	the front moves from $-1.24$ to $-0.55$---onto the tie band from below it, not
   254	above it. Its admission rate is essentially unchanged (2.44\% of cycles admit, against
   255	2.35\%). So the gate was a real cost and removing it moves the arm along the
   256	front rather than past it, which is the same trade every other route in this
   257	section makes.
   258	
   259	Across the eight \sys{} variants that left controller logs with gate state---the
   260	set is named in \texttt{gate\_attrib.csv}, and it is eight rather than nine
   261	because the ninth, \texttt{sf=0}, is the probe we report separately just above,
   262	and because \texttt{foyfix} and the two AIMD arms run a different controller and
   263	log no gate state---the projection is over budget in a median 32.2\% of blocked
   264	cycles (19.5\% to 53.6\%) and the valve in a median 32.7\% (6.8\% to 67.9\%).
   265	Both medians move with the set, which is why we name it: over every completed
   266	run in that file---forty-two of them---they are 30.6\% and 5.3\%. One
   267	convention note, because it moves
   268	these numbers: we take the median as the upper of the two middle values when
   269	the count is even (\texttt{v[len(v)//2]}, which is what
   270	\texttt{gen\_gate\_attrib.py} computes and what the file's
   271	\texttt{eight\_median} line records), not the mean of the two; on eight arms
   272	that difference is 0.4\,pp on the projection and 6.7\,pp on the valve. The round gate is
   273	off by default and binds in only two of the three arms that turn it
   274	on---\texttt{rg2} at 29.3\% and \texttt{rg3} at 18.0\% of blocked cycles,
   275	while \texttt{rg4}, the loosest of the three, stays at 0.6\% like every arm
   276	that leaves it off---so the gate
   277	binds only where it is set tightly enough to.
   278	We also record what this accounting omits, because it changes the picture: the
   279	controller has a starvation guard that bypasses the budget entirely once the
   280	oldest waiter has waited 240\,s, cooling down for 300\,s. On the base run it
   281	accounts for 24.8\% of all admissions (51 of 206), and while the projection is
   282	over budget it is the \emph{only} way in---of 3{,}700 such cycles, nine admitted,
   283	and all nine were forced. It is therefore the guard, not the budget test, that
   284	sets the floor on how fast this controller can admit.
   285	
   286	The second is that under-use does \emph{not} order the outcomes, so it is not
   287	the whole explanation either. \texttt{horizon}=1 and \texttt{cap3}+HiCache sit
   288	at the same median occupancy (57.8\% of the pool) and the same mean running
   289	count (1.52 against 1.51 on the busy-time measure of \S\ref{sec:model}), and
   290	the cap is 19 minutes faster. \texttt{hz0} runs
   291	at 64.1\%, higher than that cap, and is 54 minutes slower. Occupancy buys
   292	running requests reliably---\texttt{occupancy.csv}'s occupancy and
   293	running-count columns track
   294	each other across all twenty-three arms---but converting running requests into wall
   295	clock is not simply the per-step cost of \S\ref{sec:model}. Decomposing that
   296	19 minutes into its idle and busy components, 12.6 of them are idle time---%
   297	65\%---and only 6.8 are busy; the two runs' step costs differ by 1.2\,ms
   298	(29.2 against 28.0), about 4\%. A parked session is one the controller has
   299	authorized but not yet released, so the same policy that sets the running count
   300	also sets a large part of the idle time. Both
   301	effects are real; on this workload the unused-memory one is larger for the arms
   302	that finish worst, and the step-cost one is what separates the arms that finish
   303	close together.
   304	
   305	\subsection{What the two mechanisms explain, arm by arm}
   306	\label{sec:results:attribution}
   307	
   308	We have offered two mechanisms, and the honest thing is to say what each one
   309	covers. For every controller arm we took the static cap closest to it in wall
   310	clock and compared their occupancy and their step cost. An arm is attributed to
   311	the unused-memory mechanism if it runs fewer than 92\% of its comparison's
   312	requests, and to the step-cost mechanism if it runs a comparable number---%
   313	within 8\%---at a measurably higher cost (more than 8\% higher $\tau_1$).
   314	Everything else is a residual. The rule reads the running count and
   315	$\tau_1$ only; it does not consult occupancy, so the ``unused memory'' label
   316	is licensed by the running count alone---though the three arms it lands on also
   317	sit well below their comparison's occupancy (54.5, 56.2 and 62.1 against
   318	74.3\%), which is why we let the name stand. The comparability clause is load-bearing and we
   319	state it because an earlier version of this table did not enforce it, which
   320	classified every arm that ran \emph{more} requests than its comparison as
   321	step-cost purely because $\tau$ rises with the running count.
   322	
   323	\begin{center}
   324	\small
   325	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
   326	\begin{tabular}{lrrrl}
   327	  \toprule
   328	  Arm & Occ. & $\overline{R}$ & $\tau_1$ & Attribution \\
   329	  \midrule
   330	  single-flight off   &  54.5 & 1.455 & 28.9  & unused mem. \\
   331	  \texttt{horizon}=1  &  57.8 & 1.524 & 29.2  & residual \\
   332	  \sys{} base         &  49.2 & 1.353 & 28.7  & residual \\
   333	  \texttt{horizon}=2  &  54.7 & 1.439 & 33.6  & $\tau$ high, not matched$^\ddagger$ \\
   334	  \texttt{horizon}=0  &  64.1 & 1.666 & 35.7  & $\tau$ high, not matched$^\ddagger$ \\
   335	  \texttt{bres}       &  56.2 & 1.481 & 29.5  & unused mem. \\
   336	  \texttt{breshz1}    &  61.4 & 1.632 & 36.2  & $\tau$ high, not matched$^\ddagger$ \\
   337	  no gate, no resv.   &  62.1 & 1.678 & 31.5  & unused mem. \\
   338	  running gate$=$4    &  62.3 & 1.689 & 32.2  & $\tau$ high, not matched \\
   339	  all throttles off   &  39.2 & 1.675 & 34.8  & $\tau$ high, not matched \\
   340	  AIMD, retuned       &  66.6 & 1.706 & 34.3  & residual \\
   341	  AIMD, paper params  &  40.3 & 1.357 & 32.4  & step cost$^\ddagger$ \\
   342	  \bottomrule
   343	\end{tabular}
   344	\end{center}
   345	
   346	$^\ddagger$ marks runs measured while three or more other engine instances shared
   347	the host for at least an hour (\S\ref{sec:repro}). In this table the flag bites
   348	harder than in Table~\ref{tab:runs}, because the classification is made on
   349	$\tau_1$, which is exactly the column co-tenancy inflates. Three of the five
   350	$\tau$-high rows carry it, and so does the one step-cost attribution the
   351	procedure makes---AIMD at its published parameters. That row is flagged by the
   352	window but not measurably damaged: its $\tau_2$, which does not touch the
   353	sampler, is 24.4\,ms, inside the clean family's range. We report it as flagged
   354	rather than calling it clean, because the window is what the audit establishes
   355	and the absence of damage is an inference from a different column. The two
   356	$\tau$-high rows that are not flagged (\texttt{running gate}=4 is a watch,
   357	all-throttles-off is clean) are the ones whose elevation no window explains.
   358	
   359	4 of the 12 rows are attributed: three to unused memory (\texttt{single-flight
   360	off}, the charge-free base, and no-gate-no-reservation), one to step cost, five
   361	have a $\tau$ no
   362	matched comparison can confirm, and three are residuals---their occupancy and
   363	their step cost both sit close to their comparison cap's, and their shortfall
   364	is real but we cannot say from these measurements which mechanism produced it.
   365	Two limits on what that buys, both of which we want read before the number is
   366	quoted. First, the comparison is a static cap matched on \emph{wall clock},
   367	and the arms that finish near 260 minutes are matched against \texttt{cap5},
   368	whose SLO is 27.1\%---so the classification describes an arm relative to a
   369	wall-clock peer, not relative to the static \emph{front}, and it cannot say
   370	why the arm fails to beat the front. Second, the three unused-memory rows run
   371	fewer requests than their comparison, which is a description of what they did,
   372	not a demonstration that doing otherwise would have won; \S\ref{sec:results}
   373	is the section that establishes the loss, and this table only sorts it.
   374	We publish the table rather than a summary because a summary would claim more
   375	than we have---and because ``two mechanisms explain the negative result''
   376	would be the wrong summary. We also flag what changed here, since an earlier
   377	version of this table claimed four attributions from eleven rows and that was
   378	an artifact of two errors at once: the comparability clause above was not
   379	enforced in the code, and six arms had no $\tau_1$ because the run list behind
   380	this table was incomplete. The generator is now the only source for these rows
   381	(\texttt{attribution.csv}), it enforces the clause, and it carries the
   382	co-tenancy flag rather than having it written in by hand---which is how the
   383	earlier version came to mark two clean runs.
   384	
   385	\subsection{Route 1: the accounting base}
   386	
   387	Our controller's projection (Eq.~\ref{eq:proj}) charges a session for its full
   388	context even while it is parked in a tool wait, when its KV is evictable and
   389	therefore absent from the engine's reported occupancy. We argued this was a
   390	double charge and removed it, admitting on engine telemetry alone.
   391	
   392	The charge is not double, and removing it does what the argument predicted on
   393	the engine side: median occupancy rises from 49.2\% to 56.2\% of the pool and
   394	the busy-hour running count from 1.35 to 1.48, and the round-1+ prefix hit
   395	rate falls only from 80.6\% to 79.7\%, so no eviction spiral occurred. The run
   396	also finishes 25.4 minutes \emph{earlier} (265.9 against 291.3).
   397	
   398	It is nonetheless not a win. The SLO falls from 89.8\% to 85.9\%, and against
   399	the static front the two runs sit at the same distance---$-1.24$\,pp for the
   400	run that keeps the charge, $-1.33$\,pp for the charge-free arm, a 0.09\,pp
   401	difference against the 0.5\,pp spread the base configuration shows between its
   402	two runs. Removing the charge does not lift the controller above the envelope;
   403	it slides the operating point down along it.
   404	
   405	We want to be explicit that this is a revision. Our earlier measurement of this
   406	route had the arm 4.2 minutes \emph{slower} and 6.0\,pp worse, and we read the
   407	wall clock and the SLO as cancelling. That measurement was one of the
   408	co-tenancy-flagged runs (\S\ref{sec:repro}): its $\tau_2$ read 31.3\,ms where
   409	the same arm measured alone reads 24.8, and $\tau_2$ enters the busy time
   410	directly. Re-measured clean, the wall clock moves the other way---the arm
   411	trades 25.4 minutes for 3.9\,pp of SLO---so the cancellation was a property of
   412	the contamination, not of the mechanism. The occupancy half of the argument
   413	(49.2\% to 56.2\%) is a volume measurement and was never in doubt. We keep the
   414	route in the negative column, now on the stronger ground that the highest-leverage
   415	knob the mechanism argues for moves the arm along the front rather than over it,
   416	and we record the correction here rather than in a footnote because it is the
   417	one place in this paper where a re-run changed a conclusion's reasoning rather
   418	than its precision.
   419	
   420	We note that this claim had already been falsified once by source inspection:
   421	SGLang's \texttt{token\_usage} subtracts evictable blocks, so a parked
   422	session's prefix is genuinely not counted, and charging for it reserves
   423	capacity that really will be needed back. The end-to-end measurement agrees
   424	with the source reading.
   425	
   426	\subsection{Route 2: the growth reserve}
   427	
   428	Sessions grow by a median of 2.7K new tokens per round (\texttt{input\_len}
   429	over rounds $\ge 1$ in the trace), so the controller reserves
   430	capacity for the next \texttt{horizon} rounds. We swept the horizon over
   431	$\{0,1,2,3\}$ and the reserve is not a free parameter to be minimised:
   432	Fig.~\ref{fig:horizon} shows the response is non-monotone. Removing the
   433	reserve entirely costs 12.6\,pp of SLO, and a reserve of two rounds is
   434	15 minutes \emph{slower} than three. The optimum is at one round, and there it comes closest to the
   435	static front---0.93\,pp below it, inside the tie band---and that single point is
   436	the one this paper spends the most words on, because it does not reproduce: the
   437	repeat of this configuration lands 5.6\,pp below the line
   438	(\S\ref{sec:results}). Read the sweep as a shape, not as a result at its
   439	minimum.
   440	
   441	\subsection{Route 3: the hard ceiling}
   442	
   443	Lowering the budget fraction is the crudest available knob, and on the two
   444	settings we swept it does not behave like the others: budgets of 0.85 and 0.90
   445	finish in 333.9 and 315.9 minutes at 89.9\% and 87.7\% SLO, which is $-2.24$
   446	and $-4.22$\,pp from the envelope at those wall clocks (\texttt{budget\_sweep.csv}),
   447	against $-0.93$ and $-1.24$ for the two nearest arms at the 0.95 the rest of
   448	the paper uses. So this knob slides the point \emph{below} the front rather
   449	than along it. We report it
   450	only for completeness, since it adds no mechanism.
   451	
   452	\subsection{An independent adaptive controller}
   453	\label{sec:results:aimd}
   454	
   455	Every controller above is ours, which invites the objection that the negative
   456	result is a statement about one design rather than about adaptive admission.
   457	We therefore implemented a second, mechanistically different controller: an
   458	AIMD window over an agent-level admission set, following the control law of
   459	the congestion-based system we compare against in \S\ref{sec:related}. The
   460	window grows additively while cache occupancy sits below a low watermark, is
   461	cut multiplicatively when occupancy exceeds a high watermark \emph{and} the
   462	prefix hit rate falls below a threshold, and holds otherwise. Cuts pause the
   463	youngest sessions at their next step boundary; growth resumes paused sessions
   464	first. Every input is a runtime gauge---occupancy and hit rate from the
   465	engine, nothing from the trace---and the window acts through the same named
   466	permits as \sys{}.
   467	
   468	It does not beat the static front either, at either parameter setting
   469	(Table~\ref{tab:runs}). At the values reported in that paper's evaluation it
   470	finishes in 364.3 minutes at 81.4\% SLO, $11.2$\,pp below the envelope; the
   471	same arm with its retuned watermarks finishes in 264.6 minutes at 52.8\%,
   472	$34.3$\,pp below (\texttt{table1.csv}). The retuned setting is also one of the most
   473	volatile runs we report: its segment-to-segment SD is 13.2\,pp of SLO, with
   474	segment values swinging between 37 and 72. Two runs are more volatile
   475	(\texttt{cap4} at 15.8 and the all-throttles-off arm at 14.4), and no other arm
   476	that keeps its throttles on is as bad---which is what a control loop oscillating
   477	around a capacity cliff looks like.
   478	
   479	We report two things honestly about this comparison. First, this is our
   480	reimplementation of a published control law, not the authors' system, and a
   481	reimplementation of any controller can underperform its paper. We built it to
   482	the published law, gave it the same permit interface and the same 2\,s cycle
   483	as \sys{}, and did not tune it beyond the two documented settings; but the
   484	caveat stands. Second, both controllers stall. At its paper parameters this one pinned its
   485	window at one session, admitted nothing, and left the engine idle for over an
   486	hour. Our own controller does the same thing more briefly: in 4 of the 11
   487	\sys{} runs a scan finds windows in which the engine was idle, 85 or more
   488	sessions were waiting, the SLO valve was open and the controller issued no
   489	permit at all. Three of those runs have a single such window each---170.5, 173.2
   490	and 191.5\,s, 0.9--1.1\% of the run's wall clock---and the arm that disables
   491	every throttle has nine of them, together 12\% of its wall clock (\texttt{stall.csv} states
   492	the criterion; an earlier draft quoted 150--175\,s and 0.9\% for all four,
   493	which no version of the criterion reproduces). The remaining runs, including
   494	the best one, show no such window. Nothing is logged
   495	as an error and the run recovers on its own.
   496	
   497	We traced one of those windows to a
   498	code path that explains it: single-flight admission considered only the head of
   499	the FIFO queue, and when the head was a session too large to fit the remaining
   500	budget it was never admitted, never left the queue, and blocked every smaller
   501	session behind it. That is the code every run in this paper executed. The fix
   502	in our repository---scan the queue, still admit at most one per cycle---is
   503	\emph{not} in the artifact that produced these results, so the numbers here
   504	predate it and we do not claim them for it. We are also explicit that this is
   505	\emph{a} path, not a proven cause:
   506	the audit is a log signature, and the same signature can be produced by other
   507	closed gates. What we can say is that the signature is present in a third of
   508	our \sys{} runs and absent from all of our static caps, which have no
   509	admission decision to get stuck in.
   510	
   511	That is the honest version of a finding we first reported too strongly. An
   512	intermediate draft of this paper called it a deadlock and said the
   513	configuration that produced our best result was the one that deadlocked on its
   514	repeat. Neither holds: the windows are minutes, not hours, and they clear on their
   515	own, and the configuration that produced our best result never showed one. Nor
   516	do they move any verdict we report---0.9--1.1\% of the wall clock in each of
   517	the three runs that has a single one, and in the all-throttles-off arm 12\% of
   518	a wall clock that is already the slowest in the table. We keep them because the mechanism is real and its size
   519	is the point: on a pool that holds four median sessions, a controller's failure
   520	mode is a silent stall, not graceful degradation, and it took a repeat run to
   521	see it.
   522	
   523	\subsection{Route 4: execution granularity}
   524	\label{sec:results:gate}
   525	
   526	The first three routes all assume that what a controller can set is \emph{how
   527	many sessions are resident}. A static cap sets the same quantity, so it is
   528	unsurprising that they land in the same place. The fourth route attacks the
   529	assumption instead: because a large fraction of live sessions are parked at
   530	any instant, a cap on \emph{resident} sessions leaves the engine idle whenever
   531	all residents happen to be parked. Capping the \emph{running-request} count
   532	instead should keep the engine busy while leaving the resident count high.
   533	
   534	The permit design supports this directly: a paused session finishes its
   535	current round and blocks before its next one, and resumes when the controller
   536	re-admits it. We implemented it as a gate on the engine's own
   537	\texttt{num\_running\_reqs} and swept the gate over $\{2,4\}$. Only the
   538	gate-of-four arm ran to 998 of the trace's 999 steps; the gate-of-two arm
   539	reached 770 and is not used, so what we report below is a single comparison
   540	against the no-gate arm rather than a sweep.
   541	
   542	It does not work, and the measurement says why. Against the no-gate arm, a
   543	gate of four moved mean running requests from 1.33 to 1.30 and mean live
   544	sessions from 3.25 to 3.03: it tightened both, by 2\% and 7\%, rather than
   545	pinning the one it aims at. That is what we
   546	observe, and we state it as the observation it is rather than deriving a bound
   547	we cannot support. A live session spends most of its time parked in a tool wait
   548	(the ratio of mean running requests to mean live sessions is 0.33 to 0.64
   549	across the twenty-three arms, \texttt{occupancy.csv}'s \texttt{running\_mean}
   550	over \texttt{runs.csv}'s \texttt{conc}), so pausing live sessions removes
   551	capacity that was not running in the first place---but that ratio is an average over a run, not a
   552	bound on any instant, and the running count can in principle reach the live
   553	count. Both repairs fail: without a
   554	constraint on admission, a paused session is re-admitted on the next cycle and
   555	blocks for at most one 2\,s cycle; with one, the policy is a session cap again.
   556	The obstacle is granularity---\texttt{paused} takes effect at round
   557	boundaries, while pinning an instantaneous count requires control the
   558	permit mechanism does not have.
   559	
   560	The measured outcome matches: the gate moved running concurrency from 1.33 to
   561	1.30 and live sessions from 3.25 to 3.03. It tightened rather than pinned, and
   562	a tightening is another cap.
   563	
   564	\begin{figure}[tb]
   565	  \centering
   566	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig6_horizon.pdf}
   567	  \caption{The growth reserve is not a free parameter. Removing it costs
   568	  12.6\,pp of SLO; two rounds is 15 minutes slower than three. The optimum at
   569	  one round comes closest to the static front, 0.9\,pp below it---and is the
   570	  one point here that does not reproduce on a repeat (it lands 5.6\,pp
   571	  below).
   572	  The other three settings were each run once, so the shape is better
   573	  supported than any single point on it.}
   574	  \label{fig:horizon}
   575	\end{figure}

     1	\section{The Same Measurements on a Second Workload}
     2	\label{sec:xtrace}
     3	
     4	Everything so far is one trace family, and a negative result drawn from one
     5	family invites the objection that the family is the finding. We ran the same
     6	accounting, a static sweep from a cap of two to a cap of eight, and the same
     7	controller on a second replay:
     8	117 real Claude agent sessions, 390 rounds, the same model, the same engine and
     9	the same 101{,}432-token pool. One difference has to be stated up front,
    10	because it bears on the paper's central variable: \emph{this trace was run
    11	entirely without a host tier}. Its static points, its envelope and its
    12	controller are all device-only. That makes the comparison internally fair---both
    13	sides of the front are the same configuration---but it means this workload
    14	tests the negative result and not the tier mechanism, and it leaves an
    15	alternative account of the controller's failure standing: it failed here on the
    16	configuration where \S\ref{sec:model} says added concurrency is expensive. The two workloads differ in ways that matter for
    17	this paper, which is why we chose it. Its sessions are smaller (median prompt
    18	18{,}809 tokens against 23{,}187) and its tool waits are far longer at the
    19	median: over round transitions---same session,
    20	adjacent rounds, each session's last round excluded because it has no
    21	successor---the gap is 20.2\,s against 0.34\,s at the median---about 60$\times$---and
    22	39.9\,s against 15.8\,s at the mean, which is 2.5$\times$
    23	(\texttt{toolwait.csv} carries both traces on this definition). The 29.1\,s
    24	that appears in \texttt{elasticity\_x.csv} is the mean over all 390 rows
    25	including each session's last round, and is not comparable with the main
    26	trace's 15.8\,s; we use the transition definition throughout §\ref{sec:xtrace}. So the engine is idle far more of the
    27	time, and the running counts are lower.
    28	
    29	\subsection{What transfers, and what does not}
    30	
    31	\begin{figure}[tb]
    32	  \centering
    33	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figs/fig8_transfer.pdf}
    34	  \caption{Realization against the running count on both traces, each
    35	  normalized to its own trace's reuse bound. Device-only, the main trace falls
    36	  from 92.5\% to 36.4\% and the second from 74.8\% to 57.5\%; with a host
    37	  tier the main trace's runs stay at 72.8--92.5\%. The second trace is lower
    38	  throughout---interpolating the main trace's line at the second trace's
    39	  running counts gives a 17-point gap at the lowest and 23 at the
    40	  highest---and it declines more gently, which is what ``the direction
    41	  transfers and the level does not'' looks like on this axis.}
    42	  \label{fig:transfer}
    43	\end{figure}
    44	
    45	\paragraph{The reuse accounting transfers in direction, not in level.} The
    46	second trace plans a higher reuse bound than the first---86.3\% of its prompt
    47	tokens against 75.5\%---and realizes less of it, at a lower device occupancy
    48	(Fig.~\ref{fig:transfer}).
    49	Realization falls from 74.8\% to 57.5\% as the running count rises from 1.08 to
    50	1.50, so the direction of \S\ref{sec:model} survives. What does \emph{not}
    51	survive is the sharpness. On the main trace the decline is a threshold: the
    52	per-unit slope is $-10.4$, $-40.6$, then $-157.1$ and $-127.6$ points per unit
    53	as the running count passes 1.53, which is the cliff \S\ref{sec:criterion}
    54	measures. Here the slope stays gentler through the same region---$-31.2$,
    55	$-36.8$, $-28.4$, $-93.0$ and $-67.6$---and the two steep numbers at the end
    56	sit on spans of 0.017 and 0.074 of running count, where a difference between
    57	two runs starts to read as a trend. Taken as a whole the sweep loses 74.8--57.5\%
    58	of this trace's bound between $\overline{R}$ 1.08 and 1.50 at $-40.9$ points
    59	per unit. We do compare the two
    60	traces' overall slopes, which is the one slope comparison the data supports
    61	because both are taken over their full measured ranges
    62	(\texttt{slope.csv}): the device-only family runs from 92.5\% to 36.4\% over
    63	$\overline{R}$ 1.000--1.842, a whole-range slope of $-66.8$ points per unit,
    64	and this trace from 74.8\% to 57.5\% over 1.08--1.50, a slope of $-40.9$.
    65	So per unit of running count, the main trace loses its bound about one and a
    66	half times as fast over its range ($-66.8$ against $-40.9$)---but the
    67	comparison is between ranges of different length and
    68	different absolute level, and we do not lean on it.
    69	What this trace does establish is that the \emph{level} does not transfer, and
    70	we treat that as a bound on what we can
    71	claim: it realizes \emph{less} at 15.8\% median device occupancy than the
    72	main trace does at 22.4\%---the two numbers are the lightest cap on each trace
    73	(\texttt{realhr\_cap2} realizing 74.8\% and \texttt{cap1} realizing 92.5\%), both
    74	medians of \texttt{sglang:token\_usage} with means of 13.9\% and 25.2\%
    75	(\texttt{reuse\_x.csv}, \texttt{occupancy.csv})---so realization is not a
    76	function of occupancy alone and a
    77	rule of the form ``the pool collapses above $x$\% occupancy'' is false on our
    78	own data. Occupancy is instantaneous; reuse depends on whether a prefix
    79	survives from one round to the next, which is a history the instantaneous
    80	number does not carry. We do not measure that history here, and we say so
    81	rather than fitting the two traces to a common curve.
    82	
    83	\paragraph{The elasticity criterion transfers in sign, not in place.} On both
    84	traces the criterion plays the same role, and it is the modest one
    85	\S\ref{sec:criterion} settles on: it \emph{rules out} the workload-side
    86	story. The controller on this trace runs at a busy-time mean of 1.223, which
    87	falls inside the span its own static caps cover (1.08 to 1.50)---more
    88	precisely inside the cap3$\to$cap4 segment, where
    89	$\varepsilon(\tau_1) = -0.28$ over $\overline{R}$ 1.16--1.29, so its failure is
    90	again not explained by the criterion.
    91	What the completed sweep adds is that this trace does approach the criterion's
    92	\emph{expensive} branch, later and more gently than the main trace: above
    93	$\overline{R} \approx 1.29$ the model-consistent elasticity turns positive and
    94	touches one in the first of the three segments there ($+1.0$, then $+0.18$ and
    95	$+0.87$ as the sweep reaches 1.50), while the main trace crosses one at 1.53
    96	with $+1.15$ and stays above it. So the direction is not a property of one
    97	family; the location is. We report the point estimates and not a threshold, because a $\pm10\%$
    98	perturbation of $\tau_1$---inside the range our repeat pairs show---carries
    99	each of those three segments' bounds across zero \emph{and} across one. The per-request
   100	measure is no steadier ($+0.45$, $-1.25$, $+0.73$). And the controller never
   101	operates where the branch turns expensive: 1.22, below 1.29 and below the main
   102	trace's 1.53 alike.
   103	
   104	We also record what the noise does, because it is the same weakness the whole
   105	paper has. Recomputing each segment's $\varepsilon$ with $\tau_1$ perturbed
   106	by $\pm10$\%---against the 0.3--1.1\% our clean repeat pairs differ by in
   107	$\tau_1$ (\texttt{decomp.csv}; one anomalous pair reaches 42.5\%), so the
   108	perturbation is generous rather than conservative---gives bands of
   109	$[-5.20, +0.41]$ for cap2$\to$cap3,
   110	$[-2.17, +1.61]$ for cap3$\to$cap4, $[-1.25, +3.26]$ for cap4$\to$cap5,
   111	$[-14.07, +14.43]$ for cap5$\to$cap6 and $[-3.33, +5.07]$ for
   112	cap6$\to$cap8. Every one of them crosses zero, and the three at the end cross
   113	one as well. So at that perturbation no segment's sign is resolvable, and the
   114	turn the paragraph above describes is a property of the point estimates rather
   115	than a boundary we have located---which is a bound on that paragraph, not a
   116	reprieve from it.
   117	
   118	\paragraph{The controller's verdict transfers, weakly.} \sys{} finishes the
   119	second trace in 75.6 minutes, against 66.2 for the fastest static run on it
   120	(the cap of eight), so \sys{} is 14.3\% slower (14.26\% at full precision).
   121	We do not report that as a loss. The repeat pairs we have vary by 1.7--4.7\%
   122	in wall clock (\texttt{replicates.csv}), and this trace gives us two of its
   123	own: the cap of four repeats to within 1.7\%, and \sys{} itself to within
   124	0.06\% in wall clock and 0.03 points of realization. The gap is more than
   125	eight times the wider of those, so it is not repeat noise on either side of
   126	it---but two pairs cannot bound the variability of the \emph{other}
   127	configurations in the comparison, and run-to-run spread on this machine
   128	reaches 22.0\% across the
   129	other configurations and tiers we have measured (\texttt{repeat\_spread.csv};
   130	0.0--6.1\% across the ten pairs those two traces give us, the spread between
   131	the pairs themselves being the reason we report a range and not a number).
   132	So the honest reading is the one we have used throughout: at the threshold
   133	this paper uses, it did not win on
   134	either axis.
   135	Its signed distance from the second trace's
   136	static envelope is $+0.40$\,pp at $10$\,s on
   137	the SLO axis---inside the $\pm1.0$\,pp tie band, which is defined on that
   138	axis---and $-0.14$\,pp on the cache-hit axis, against the 14.9 points that
   139	separate this trace's own six static caps on that axis. That axis is
   140	threshold-dependent here, and in the opposite direction from the main trace's:
   141	at $5$\,s this trace's caps separate much more (the cap of eight scores
   142	$78.2$\% against the cap of two's $99.0$\%) and the controller sits
   143	$+1.87$\,pp above the envelope---outside the band, a win on that axis---whereas
   144	on the main trace no controller reaches the front at $5$\,s and one crosses at
   145	$20$\,s (\S\ref{sec:discussion}). We report both readings rather than the one
   146	that gives a single answer. (The envelope and the
   147	elasticity below are both built from all six static caps the sweep now has;
   148	the three caps the sweep added after the first version of this comparison---the
   149	caps of five, six and eight---are the reason the last three segments exist
   150	at all.)
   151	The SLO number is nearly uninformative, and we flag that rather than let it
   152	stand as a measurement. At the three caps the envelope was originally built
   153	from, every point on this trace scored 99.5--100.0\%, so the axis could not
   154	discriminate at all. The three the sweep has since added do separate---the cap
   155	of eight scores 93.8\% against the cap of two's 100.0\%---so the axis is not
   156	flat after all, but its whole spread over six static caps is 6.2 points against
   157	a $\pm1.0$\,pp tie band, and \sys{} sits at 100.0\% on it.
   158	
   159	\subsection{What this section does and does not buy}
   160	
   161	It buys a negative result that is not a property of one trace family: on a
   162	second workload, with a different session-size distribution and a
   163	tool-wait distribution about 60$\times$ longer at the median, the same controller
   164	still does not beat the same kind of static cap, and the reuse degradation
   165	still appears. It costs us the transportability of the threshold. On the main trace the
   166	criterion narrowed the search by excluding the workload-side explanation and
   167	put the turn of the point estimates at $\overline{R} \approx 1.53$; on this
   168	trace the same measurement turns positive above 1.29. What we cannot do is
   169	\emph{locate} either turn---the $\pm10\%$ perturbation puts every segment's
   170	bounds across zero on both traces---which is the caveat
   171	\S\ref{sec:criterion} already states for the main trace, now with a second
   172	trace behind it. The controller is below both turns (1.22), so the criterion
   173	does not explain its failure on either trace. We
   174	report both, and we note that the second workload is closer to the regime
   175	where adaptive control \emph{should} pay---lower concurrency, much longer idle
   176	gaps---and the controller still did not win there.
